[1992-02-17T17:51:16+00:00] [thorinn@diku.dk (Lars Henrik Mathiesen)] [jch@mitchell.cit.cornell.edu (Jeffrey C Honig) writes:
>The common portion of a backtrace in several dumps are:

>_Xtransflt(3e30333c,2039323a,802e7580,802d8f80) at _Xtransflt+1d
>_nfs_request(802d5400,802e3680,e83e0100,4,8007e734,0,802d6e00,7ffffdd4,7ffffdd0,7ffffde4) at _nfs_request+1c4
>_nfs_lookup(802d5400,7fffe6cc) at _nfs_lookup+3ca
>_namei(7fffe6cc) at _namei+27e

>The code at _nfs_request+1c4 is very close to the call to nfs_send()
>so the fault may be occuring in nfs_send().

Well, adb tends to believe the VAX stack frames blindly, and it gets
the routine names at the start of each line from the next frame's
return address (which is repeated at the end). When a fault (or
interrupt) occurs, control moves to the handler without the benefit of
a stack frame, and adb mixes up the return address in the handler with
the stack frame of the routine where the trap happened.

If the translation fault did occur in nfs_send (i.e., nfs_request+1c4
is the instruction after the call), then the first two arguments
should have been the pointers nmp->nm_so and nmp->nm_nam, but they
seem to be longwords containing the ASCII fragments "<30>" and ":29 "
--- either something overwrote the nfsmount structure, or it was freed
and reused. (The two words are at offsets 12 and 28 in the structure,
and the ASCII stuff could very well be from an mbuf with a syslog
message (daemon.info).)

Were you trying to access something that the system had let you
umount? If so, it would seem that it leaves dangling references
around.

Lars Mathiesen (U of Copenhagen CS Dep) <thorinn@diku.dk> (Humour NOT marked)

[1992-03-01T07:50:51+00:00] [anthony@csd4.csd.uwm.edu (Anthony J Stieber)] [In article <1992Feb29.204246.11566@sunb10.cs.uiuc.edu> cs318a52@ibmb5.cs.uiuc.edu (Mark Lanett) writes:
>jim@ferkel.ucsb.edu (Jim Lick) writes:
>>The other future development is the Gnu OS based on their 'Hurd'
>>kernel.  No telling when this will be out though.
>Actually, the "GNU OS" will be a gnu filesystem (HURD) running on Mach
>(the unix successor being developed by CMU).

Actually, the GNU Hurd is neither a kernel, nor just a filesystem.  It
is a Hird of Unix Replacing Daemons (among other things) that include a
filesystem, ttys, and exec, proc, and authorization servers that the
Mach kernel does not provide itself.  Mach is by itself just a nifty
little kernel and doesn't provide much of the functionality needed for
modern unix operating systems.

See ftp.gnu.ai.mit.edu for the GNU Hurd sources.
--
<-:(= Anthony Stieber	anthony@csd4.csd.uwm.edu   uwm!uwmcsd4!anthony

[1992-03-12T22:45:17+00:00] [jdi@franz.com (John D. Irwin)] [Here are the release notes for the 386BSD 0.0 distribution.  Please
note the warnings that this is a very experimental release.  For
example it cannot coexist with DOS at all (Unix takes over the whole
physical hard disk).

We're working on putting the distribution on an ftp site; it's slightly
complicated since there are both 3+1/2 inch and 5+1/4 inch distributions,
in a raw binary (compressed tar) format, as well as a binary boot floppy.
You should see an announcement soon.

	-- John

PS: I have no relationship with the release other than shlepping myself
    down to Cupertino to scarf it from John Sokol.

-- slice --

                  Release Notes on 386BSD
                         W. Jolitz


386BSD Release 0.0:
------ ------- - -

     This  is 386BSD Release 0.0, the first edition from the
386BSD project.  It comprises an entire and  complete  UNIX-
like  operating system for the Intel 80386/486 based IBM PC,
and is based almost entirely on the NET/2 release  from  the
University  of  California, which contained much of the ear-
lier freely redistributable  and  modifiable  386BSD  source
code  done  by William F. Jolitz and contributed to the Uni-
versity of California at Berkeley for distribution.

     Originally conceived by Bill and Lynne Jolitz in  1989,
the  386BSD project is an attempt to foster new research and
development in operating systems and  networking  technology
by broadening access to base technology. In cooperation with
the University of California, an advanced  operating  system
was  redesigned  by  William  F.  Jolitz  to  work on common
386-based PC's for use by smaller colleges and other  groups
that  did  not have the resources to otherwise obtain it. In
addition, starting with the NET/2 release, this software has
been released in a form that does not require license agree-
ments, non-disclosure, or other controls  that  would  limit
it's use in undergraduate teaching programs.

     Unlike  NET/2,  386BSD  Release  0.0  is a complete and
operational system, including  binaries  and  an  executable
installation  system,  but  still  available  under the same
"freely  redistributable"  terms  of  the   original   NET/2
release.  Our  forthcoming  book  on the internals of 386BSD
will complete the picture for educational and research  pro-
grams  to make use of this technology with students with the
necessary academic freedom.

     We have been writing a series of articles about  386BSD
that  have  appeared  in  Dr. Dobbs Journal since January of
                          --  ----- -------
1991. Future announcements, and information on 386BSD may be
found  within  its covers. The DDJ BBS should have copies of
binary and source code when available. Also, you can contact
us via the magazine.

Contents:
--------

     Release 0.0 consists of:

Source Distribution
     A  collection  of  8  or  10 high-density floppy disks,
     which is a multi-volume compressed TAR  format  archive
     of the source language files with which to recreate the
     system.    When   extracted,    the    files    consume


386BSD RELEASE NOTES          1                   March 1992










     approximately  31 MB of space. In addition, at least 28
     MB of space is taken up by  the  binary  files  created
     when recompiling.

Binary Distribution
     A  collection of 6 or 8 high-density floppy disks, also
     in compressed multi-volume  TAR  form,  containing  the
     executable,  data, and documentation files of a working
     386BSD  system,  including  C  and  C++  compilers  and
     libraries.   When  extracted, the files occupy approxi-
     mately 20 MB of disk space. Note that at least 5 MB  of
     swap space, plus an operating reserve of another 10% of
     the total accumulated disk space  mentioned  should  be
     considered as minimum to operate this system.

Distribution Installation Floppy System
     A  single  floppy  system is provided, again on a high-
     density diskette.  This  completely  standalone  system
     manages  to  allow  a  potential 386/486 based PC to be
     qualified for use with 386BSD, simply by attempting  to
     boot it as an ordinary floppy. Once operational, it can
     be used to configure the PC's hard disk  and  load  the
     binary  floppy  distribution.  In addition, this floppy
     provides a means to rescue and repair the  software  on
     the hard disk in the event of a calamity.

Difference Floppy
     A  single  360 KB MS-DOS floppy containing all the dif-
     ferences and new files necessary to make the NET/2 tape
     operational,  for  those  who already have the tape and
     wish to "do it themselves". It also  serves  to  illus-
     trate  just  what  is  necessary to make the NET/2 tape
     usable and worthwhile.

     Release 0.0 does not contain any proprietary code,  nor
any  encryption software. It was created from NET/2, GNU and
other public software, and our creative minds.

Scope and Goals of this Release:
----- --- ----- -- ---- -------

     This release was motivated by the fact that  access  to
386BSD  has not been provided to all interested parties on a
timely basis by the University or other sources, as  we  had
originally  intended.   Thus, we have done a minimalist ver-
sion to demonstrate feasibility, provide accessibility,  and
assure  our readers and supporters that this project will be
finished, available to all, and  not  just  appropriated  by
private  concerns.   Since  it is minimalist by design, many
features, utilities and other functionality will  be  desir-
able  to  add,  although the system is complete enough to be
self-sufficient and self-developing.




386BSD RELEASE NOTES          2                   March 1992










     In addition, we have not repaired numerous  known  bugs
present  -- we have merely attempted to work around them and
in spite of them. Also, new  subsystems  created  after  the
NET/2  tape  and contributed to Berkeley have not been added
back in, because we did not want to blur the distinctions of
what is required to make NET/2 operational, and because CSRG
will not allow us access to this contributed work,  although
other  groups  have  been  allowed  access.  Future releases
hopefully will remedy these nuisances. We  also  expect  the
involvement  of  a  wider  community of users will aid us in
improving future releases of 386BSD.

Devices Supported in this Release:
------- --------- -- ---- -------

     This release is intended to support a  minimal  386/486
SX/DX  ISA(ATBUS)  system,  with  the  traditional  hard and
floppy disk controller (MFM,ESDI,IDE).  Also, the usual dis-
play  adapters  (MDA/CGA/VGA/HGC)  are supported, along with
the communications ports (COM).  Ethernet  controllers  sup-
ported   are    Western   Digital  8003EB,  8003EBT,  8003S,
WD8003SBT, 8013EBT, and Novell NE2000. Clones also appear to
work quite well.  Tape drive support is available for QIC-02
controllers as well, allowing use of 3M cartridges of QIC-60
through QIC-150 format.

     As  configured  on  the binary distribution, the system
requires a floating point coprocessor  (387  of  any  make),
hard  disk and controller, floppy disk drive (either 5.25 or
3.5 high density only), and display adapter. If  the  serial
port  or a Western Digital Ethernet card (port 0x280, IRQ 3,
iomem 0xd000) is present, the system can make use of  it  as
well.

     It  is recommended that the system have at least 2MB of
memory or more, but it will run on much smaller systems to a
limited  degree  by paging (the C++ compiler uses about 1 MB
of memory in operation).  A 4 MB system with an 200 MB+  IDE
disk is a comfortable configuration, although by sharing the
sources via NFS, networked systems with  40  MB  drives  are
quite useful.

Machines Tested:
-------- ------

     At  the  moment,  this software has only been tested on
the following configurations:

Toshiba laptop clone, 386SX/387SX, 3MB RAM, VGA LCD(Cirrus),
     Megahertz T2LL Ethernet, Conners CP3100 IDE 100MB drive.

Compaq DeskPro, 386/387, 9MB RAM, Compaq VGA, ESDI Maxtor 8380 drive(type 38),
     WD8003EBT Ethernet, Compaq QIC-150 cartridge drive.




386BSD RELEASE NOTES          3                   March 1992










     Please fill out the enclosed registration form and sur-
vey so we can add more to this test base list. It is expect-
ed that all Compaq, Toshiba, Chips and Technology-based, and
OPTI-based  systems should work with little trouble. See up-
coming DDJ articles on installation troubles for further in-
fo.   We  can be contacted for limited help with the system,
but, realize that this work is currently unfunded and we can
only devote a tiny amount of time to it. As a hint to fixing
troubles, defeating options like shadow ram or RAM  BIOS  is
an excellent place to start.

Installation Procedures:
------------ ----------

     Currently, the system does not coexist with MS-DOS, but
requires the entire machine. SINCE IT IS STILL  EXPERIMENTAL
SOFTWARE,  YOU  SHOULD  BE  PREPARED AT ANYTIME TO LOSE DISK
DRIVE CONTENTS, so you had better save backup dumps  offline
of any information you wish to be preserved (1 in 100 of you
will do this, of course, but you were warned).

     First, make a copy of all of  the  diskettes  and  save
them away. Make many copies of the distribution installation
diskette, and salt them away in various places, as it is im-
possible to recover the system without one otherwise.

     The  distribution  installation diskette has predefined
shell variables that correspond to the device name  for  the
floppy  drive  ($FD), the raw device name ($RFD), the amount
of storage ($FTRK) per track (in kilobytes), and the disktab
entry type ($FT). These are present to parameterize the dif-
ferences between 3.5 inch and 5.25 inch versions of the boot
floppy.

     Format  the  drive, if it is not already. Determine the
geometry (sectors/track, tracks/cylinder, number  of  cylin-
ders,  etc),  and create a disktab entry describing the disk
drive in the /etc/disktab file on the floppy.  This  can  be
done by allowing the floppy to be written to (it defaults to
disabling writing) by the command:

mount -u $FD /

     You can edit the file with the elvis editor, a clone of
                                    -----
the  encumbered  Berkeley vi text editor done by Steve Kirk-
                          --
endall.  You may wish to use one of the existing disktab en-
tries  as  a template for a new entry you are making. Please
include any disktab entrys you make in the survey form so we
can  include them in the next release.  Note: after you have
written the disk, please execute the "sync" command so  that
the file will be forced back to the diskette.

     Next,  use  the  disklabel command to write a bootstrap
                      ---------
and disk label data structure on the hard disk itself.  This


386BSD RELEASE NOTES          4                   March 1992










will destroy any information present on the beginning cylin-
der of the hard disk drive. A sample disklabel command:

disklabel -r -w wd0 cp3100    (label winchester drive 0 as a conners 3100)
disklabel -r wd0         (display the label on winchester drive 0)


     Next, create empty filesystems for the root  (partition
a  of  the  drive) and usr (partition h) with the newfs com-
                                                  -----
mand:

newfs wd0a     (root partition high level format)
newfs wd0h     (usr partition high level format)


     Mount the root partition and transfer the  contents  of
the distribution installation floppy to the hard drive. This
step frees up the floppy for use in loading the multi-volume
distribution while running off the hard disk drive:

mount /dev/wd0a /mnt  (associate the mnt directory with the new root filesystem)
(cd /; tar -cf - .) | (cd /mnt; tar -xf -)   (copy floppy to hard disk)
sync                          (flush out written blocks)

     Reboot  the  system  by  the  traditional cntrl-alt-del
three-fingered reset, and  remove  the  floppy  and  set  it
aside.  The  system  should  now  come  up off the hard disk
drive. Next, we load the distribution by inserting the first
floppy (volume 1 of the binary distribution) and typing:

mount /dev/wd0h /usr (make usr filesystem available, as it will also be loaded)
mr $FTRK $RFD | tar -xzf -  (floppy extract compressed floppy archive)


     A prompt will ask for successive floppies to be insert-
ed into the drive.  At the conclusion,  the  "sync"  command
should be used, and the system rebooted. The installation is
now complete, and the same procedures may be  used  at  this
point to extract the source distribution if desired.

Operation:
---------

     At  the  moment,  386BSD  comes up single user, and re-
quires manual starting of the system  daemons,  as  well  as
filesystem  checks. In use, one would minimally wish to type
after booting:

fsck -p
mount -a
update
/etc/netstart




386BSD RELEASE NOTES          5                   March 1992










     This will improve in forthcoming  versions  of  386BSD.
Be  aware  that  the  user is running as the super-user, and
care should be taken given the maximum privileges present.

Known bugs:
----- ----

     Many bugs and unimplemented portions of the system  ex-
ist  and  can  be  annoying. The most irritating are the tty
driver bugs that are  related  to  boundaries  in  the  ring
buffers,  which cause the input queue to become truncated or
wrapped, as well as freezing the input queue when a  transi-
tion  to  RAW  mode  occurs  near a boundary (generally, the
first time the command  "more"  prompts).  Usually,  hitting
control-C clears this situation.

     Sometimes,  an  endless  end-of-file  on input from the
terminal occurs that may require the  system  to  be  reset.
There  is  a pipe bug, believed to be in the block I/O code,
that breaks large pipe transfers into ~3  KB  maximum  sized
chunks.   Occasionally,  a  missing interrupt bug causes the
system to  jam  waiting  for  an  interrupt  that  has  been
botched.   Init  does  not handle signals and process groups
correctly, nor does it support multiuser operation (you  can
start  up other users by hand, or over the network as incom-
ing terminal sessions!).  Execve will not run shell scripts,
nor  will it work with arglists greater than 2 KB.  There is
no facility for program debugging (e.g.  ptrace).   Raw  DMA
transfers  to non-page aligned, non-consecutive within 64 KB
physical boundaries don't work correctly.  The console  ter-
minal emulator destroys screen contents occasionally.  A re-
dundant swap free fragment bug is  present  under  intensive
paging operations, and resource constipation due to hundreds
of processes on tiny machines does occur.  Operation on less
than  2MB  may  be  erratic or impossible due to a base page
memory botch present.

     All of these bugs are understood. Some of the fixes re-
quire  redesign  while  others require code from the article
series!, but we are sure more are present.  Bug  fixes  will
be put into subsequent versions.

Key Missing Utilities:
--- ------- ---------

     Among  the  most  annoying  missing utilities are: awk,
grep, sort, diff, test, and expr.  The utility software  has
been cudgeled to ignore these for the moment, but eventually
these must be rectified. All of  the  NET/2  utilities  have
been  made  to work with 386BSD, including those not present
in this release due to space considerations. You  will  find
it  fairly  painless  to  add  software to this base system,
which is still at heart a full 32-bit POSIX compliant  oper-
ating system with program development environment.



386BSD RELEASE NOTES          6                   March 1992










     Note  also  that  DES encryption and Kerberos have pur-
posely been left off the system to allow  for  international
use, as 386BSD incorporates software (such as NFS) which has
been done by researchers in other countries and  contributed
to  Berkeley.  For those international readers who have con-
sidered obtaining the NET/2 tape from UCB, it might interest
them  to know that an export license (GTDA) has been granted
for a version of the NET/2 tape.

Future
------

     Your interest, involvement, and support in this project
and  its  goals will determine the future of 386BSD and suc-
cessive releases. We would like to take this  much  further,
but  we  need  considerable assistance of all kinds to allow
386BSD to grow further. We realize the shortcomings  of  Re-
lease  0.0,  but  are intensely proud of what we have accom-
plished in providing you with a chance  to  become  involved
with a system that has enough tools to develop itself.




































386BSD RELEASE NOTES          7                   March 1992

     operational,  for  those  who already have the tape and
     wish to "do it themselves". It also  serves  to  illus-
     trate  just  what  is  necessary to make the NET/2 tape
     usable and worthwhile.

     Release 0.0 does not contain any proprietary code,  nor
any  encryption software. It was created from NET/2, GNU and
other public software, and our creative minds.

_S_c_o_p_e _a_n_d _G_o_a_l_s _o_f _t_h_i_s _R_e_l_e_a_s_e:

     This release was motivated by the fact that  access  to
386BSD  has not been provided to all interested parties on a
timely basis by the University or other sources, as  we  had
originally  intended.   Thus, we have done a minimalist ver-
sion to demonstrate feasibility, provide accessibility,  and
assure  our readers and supporters that this project will be
finished, available to all, and  not  just  appropriated  by
private  concerns.   Since  it is minimalist by design, many
features, utilities and other functionality will  be  desir-
able  to  add,  although the system is complete enough to be
self-sufficient and self-developing.




338866BBSSDD RREELLEEAASSEE NNOOTTEESS          22                   MMaarrcchh 11999922










     In addition, we have not repaired numerous  known  bugs
present  -- we have merely attempted to work around them and
in spite of them. Also, new  subsystems  created  after  the
NET/2  tape  and contributed to Berkeley have not been added
back in, because we did not want to blur the distinctions of
what is required to make NET/2 operational, and because CSRG
will not allow us access to this contributed work,  although
other  groups  have  been  allowed  access.  Future releases
hopefully will remedy these nuisances. We  also  expect  the
involvement  of  a  wider  community of users will aid us in
improving future releases of 386BSD.

_D_e_v_i_c_e_s _S_u_p_p_o_r_t_e_d _i_n _t_h_i_s _R_e_l_e_a_s_e:

     This release is intended to support a  minimal  386/486
SX/DX  ISA(ATBUS)  system,  with  the  traditional  hard and
floppy disk controller (MFM,ESDI,IDE).  Also, the usual dis-
play  adapters  (MDA/CGA/VGA/HGC)  are supported, along with
the communications ports (COM).  Ethernet  controllers  sup-
ported   are    Western   Digital  8003EB,  8003EBT,  8003S,
WD8003SBT, 8013EBT, and Novell NE2000. Clones also appear to
work quite well.  Tape drive support is available for QIC-02
controllers as well, allowing use of 3M cartridges of QIC-60
through QIC-150 format.

     As  configured  on  the binary distribution, the system
requires a floating point coprocessor  (387  of  any  make),
hard  disk and controller, floppy disk drive (either 5.25 or
3.5 high density only), and display adapter. If  the  serial
port  or a Western Digital Ethernet card (port 0x280, IRQ 3,
iomem 0xd000) is present, the system can make use of  it  as
well.

     It  is recommended that the system have at least 2MB of
memory or more, but it will run on much smaller systems to a
limited  degree  by paging (the C++ compiler uses about 1 MB
of memory in operation).  A 4 MB system with an 200 MB+  IDE
disk is a comfortable configuration, although by sharing the
sources via NFS, networked systems with  40  MB  drives  are
quite useful.

_M_a_c_h_i_n_e_s _T_e_s_t_e_d:

     At  the  moment,  this software has only been tested on
the following configurations:

Toshiba laptop clone, 386SX/387SX, 3MB RAM, VGA LCD(Cirrus),
     Megahertz T2LL Ethernet, Conners CP3100 IDE 100MB drive.

Compaq DeskPro, 386/387, 9MB RAM, Compaq VGA, ESDI Maxtor 8380 drive(type 38),
     WD8003EBT Ethernet, Compaq QIC-150 cartridge drive.




338866BBSSDD RREELLEEAASSEE NNOOTTEESS          33                   MMaarrcchh 11999922










     Please fill out the enclosed registration form and sur-
vey so we can add more to this test base list. It is expect-
ed that all Compaq, Toshiba, Chips and Technology-based, and
OPTI-based  systems should work with little trouble. See up-
coming DDJ articles on installation troubles for further in-
fo.   We  can be contacted for limited help with the system,
but, realize that this work is currently unfunded and we can
only devote a tiny amount of time to it. As a hint to fixing
troubles, defeating options like shadow ram or RAM  BIOS  is
an excellent place to start.

_I_n_s_t_a_l_l_a_t_i_o_n _P_r_o_c_e_d_u_r_e_s:

     Currently, the system does not coexist with MS-DOS, but
requires the entire machine. SINCE IT IS STILL  EXPERIMENTAL
SOFTWARE,  YOU  SHOULD  BE  PREPARED AT ANYTIME TO LOSE DISK
DRIVE CONTENTS, so you had better save backup dumps  offline
of any information you wish to be preserved (1 in 100 of you
will do this, of course, but you were warned).

     First, make a copy of all of  the  diskettes  and  save
them away. Make many copies of the distribution installation
diskette, and salt them away in various places, as it is im-
possible to recover the system without one otherwise.

     The  distribution  installation diskette has predefined
shell variables that correspond to the device name  for  the
floppy  drive  ($FD), the raw device name ($RFD), the amount
of storage ($FTRK) per track (in kilobytes), and the disktab
entry type ($FT). These are present to parameterize the dif-
ferences between 3.5 inch and 5.25 inch versions of the boot
floppy.

     Format  the  drive, if it is not already. Determine the
geometry (sectors/track, tracks/cylinder, number  of  cylin-
ders,  etc),  and create a disktab entry describing the disk
drive in the /etc/disktab file on the floppy.  This  can  be
done by allowing the floppy to be written to (it defaults to
disabling writing) by the command:

mount -u $FD /

     You can edit the file with the _e_l_v_i_s editor, a clone of
the  encumbered  Berkeley _v_i text editor done by Steve Kirk-
endall.  You may wish to use one of the existing disktab en-
tries  as  a template for a new entry you are making. Please
include any disktab entrys you make in the survey form so we
can  include them in the next release.  Note: after you have
written the disk, please execute the "sync" command so  that
the file will be forced back to the diskette.

     Next,  use  the  _d_i_s_k_l_a_b_e_l command to write a bootstrap
and disk label data structure on the hard disk itself.  This


338866BBSSDD RREELLEEAASSEE NNOOTTEESS          44                   MMaarrcchh 11999922










will destroy any information present on the beginning cylin-
der of the hard disk drive. A sample disklabel command:

disklabel -r -w wd0 cp3100    (label winchester drive 0 as a conners 3100)
disklabel -r wd0         (display the label on winchester drive 0)


     Next, create empty filesystems for the root  (partition
a  of  the  drive) and usr (partition h) with the _n_e_w_f_s com-
mand:

newfs wd0a     (root partition high level format)
newfs wd0h     (usr partition high level format)


     Mount the root partition and transfer the  contents  of
the distribution installation floppy to the hard drive. This
step frees up the floppy for use in loading the multi-volume
distribution while running off the hard disk drive:

mount /dev/wd0a /mnt  (associate the mnt directory with the new root filesystem)
(cd /; tar -cf - .) | (cd /mnt; tar -xf -)   (copy floppy to hard disk)
sync                          (flush out written blocks)

     Reboot  the  system  by  the  traditional cntrl-alt-del
three-fingered reset, and  remove  the  floppy  and  set  it
aside.  The  system  should  now  come  up off the hard disk
drive. Next, we load the distribution by inserting the first
floppy (volume 1 of the binary distribution) and typing:

mount /dev/wd0h /usr (make usr filesystem available, as it will also be loaded)
mr $FTRK $RFD | tar -xzf -  (floppy extract compressed floppy archive)


     A prompt will ask for successive floppies to be insert-
ed into the drive.  At the conclusion,  the  "sync"  command
should be used, and the system rebooted. The installation is
now complete, and the same procedures may be  used  at  this
point to extract the source distribution if desired.

_O_p_e_r_a_t_i_o_n:

     At  the  moment,  386BSD  comes up single user, and re-
quires manual starting of the system  daemons,  as  well  as
filesystem  checks. In use, one would minimally wish to type
after booting:

fsck -p
mount -a
update
/etc/netstart




338866BBSSDD RREELLEEAASSEE NNOOTTEESS          55                   MMaarrcchh 11999922










     This will improve in forthcoming  versions  of  386BSD.
Be  aware  that  the  user is running as the super-user, and
care should be taken given the maximum privileges present.

_K_n_o_w_n _b_u_g_s:

     Many bugs and unimplemented portions of the system  ex-
ist  and  can  be  annoying. The most irritating are the tty
driver bugs that are  related  to  boundaries  in  the  ring
buffers,  which cause the input queue to become truncated or
wrapped, as well as freezing the input queue when a  transi-
tion  to  RAW  mode  occurs  near a boundary (generally, the
first time the command  "more"  prompts).  Usually,  hitting
control-C clears this situation.

     Sometimes,  an  endless  end-of-file  on input from the
terminal occurs that may require the  system  to  be  reset.
There  is  a pipe bug, believed to be in the block I/O code,
that breaks large pipe transfers into ~3  KB  maximum  sized
chunks.   Occasionally,  a  missing interrupt bug causes the
system to  jam  waiting  for  an  interrupt  that  has  been
botched.   Init  does  not handle signals and process groups
correctly, nor does it support multiuser operation (you  can
start  up other users by hand, or over the network as incom-
ing terminal sessions!).  Execve will not run shell scripts,
nor  will it work with arglists greater than 2 KB.  There is
no facility for program debugging (e.g.  ptrace).   Raw  DMA
transfers  to non-page aligned, non-consecutive within 64 KB
physical boundaries don't work correctly.  The console  ter-
minal emulator destroys screen contents occasionally.  A re-
dundant swap free fragment bug is  present  under  intensive
paging operations, and resource constipation due to hundreds
of processes on tiny machines does occur.  Operation on less
than  2MB  may  be  erratic or impossible due to a base page
memory botch present.

     All of these bugs are understood. Some of the fixes re-
quire  redesign  while  others require code from the article
series!, but we are sure more are present.  Bug  fixes  will
be put into subsequent versions.

_K_e_y _M_i_s_s_i_n_g _U_t_i_l_i_t_i_e_s:

     Among  the  most  annoying  missing utilities are: awk,
grep, sort, diff, test, and expr.  The utility software  has
been cudgeled to ignore these for the moment, but eventually
these must be rectified. All of  the  NET/2  utilities  have
been  made  to work with 386BSD, including those not present
in this release due to space considerations. You  will  find
it  fairly  painless  to  add  software to this base system,
which is still at heart a full 32-bit POSIX compliant  oper-
ating system with program development environment.



338866BBSSDD RREELLEEAASSEE NNOOTTEESS          66                   MMaarrcchh 11999922










     Note  also  that  DES encryption and Kerberos have pur-
posely been left off the system to allow  for  international
use, as 386BSD incorporates software (such as NFS) which has
been done by researchers in other countries and  contributed
to  Berkeley.  For those international readers who have con-
sidered obtaining the NET/2 tape from UCB, it might interest
them  to know that an export license (GTDA) has been granted
for a version of the NET/2 tape.

_F_u_t_u_r_e

     Your interest, involvement, and support in this project
and  its  goals will determine the future of 386BSD and suc-
cessive releases. We would like to take this  much  further,
but  we  need  considerable assistance of all kinds to allow
386BSD to grow further. We realize the shortcomings  of  Re-
lease  0.0,  but  are intensely proud of what we have accom-
plished in providing you with a chance  to  become  involved
with a system that has enough tools to develop itself.




































338866BBSSDD RREELLEEAASSEE NNOOTTEESS          77                   MMaarrcchh 11999922



-- 
Richard Foulk		richard@pegasus.com

#! /bin/sh
# This is a shell archive.  Remove anything before this line, then unpack
# it by saving it into a file and typing "sh file".  To overwrite existing
# files, type "sh file -c".  You can also feed this as standard input via
# unshar, or by typing "sh <file", e.g..  If this archive is complete, you

X
Xget the sources for /usr/bin/gprof and /usr/sbin/kgmon from ftp.uu.net
X(or wherever), put them in the tree, and make "obj" links and dirs.
END_OF_FILE

the lack of a socket called /dev/printer?  If so, how does one

[1992-04-10T20:44:38+00:00] [karl@NeoSoft.com (Karl Lehenbauer)] [We're running NFS under 386BSD here at NeoSoft, and it's working great.

The NFS option needs to be defined in the configuration file, as you've
done.  Then look at /etc/rc -- you need to run some of the commands in
there to get the network and NFS daemons fired up.  

Note that the default shell doesn't recognize the "if [ ]" syntax.  You'll
have to use bash or modify the script to do "if test ..." instead.

[1992-04-29T20:17:51+00:00] [jc@sppip7.lkg.dec.com (John Chambers)] [In article <1992Apr21.191506.28334@chinet.chi.il.us>, les@chinet.chi.il.us (Leslie Mikesell) writes:
> >>Well, looking in the 1.03 release of the Taylor-UUCP code, one finds the
> >>[...]No uugetty.
> 
> >Too true.  Probably won't ever be one, either.
> 
> Too bad. It's time someone added some intelligence to the program to
> handle  things  like logging caller ID, auto-fax-detection, and even
> mundane speed switching to match what the modem is  doing  when  the
> interface speed is not locked.  To do that you need to listen to the
> modem *before* carrier detect comes up, and if you are  doing  that,
> you  have  to  coordinate  closely  with  dial-out programs to avoid
> interferring with them.

Hmmm...I just  got  ahold  of  the  Taylor  UUCP.   Perhaps  I  should
contribute  my  "uutty"  program to the package.  It does most of what
uugetty does (but it doesn't have auto-fax-detection, and  I  have  no
clue  as  to how I might add such a thing).  It has been posted in the
sources group a couple of times, though my own version has  bug  fixes
for a lot more systems now.

I've often suggested to people that they write their own login daemon.
It's  a really good learning experience.  Of course, most people would
rather not have to learn some of the things that you have to learn  to
make  such  a beastie work.  For instance, one thing that's a constant
headache is that when I port it to a new machine and  log  in  through
it, csh never works. There are some strict requirements for the port's
setting in csh, and they aren't documented anywhere, and if you  don't
get it exactly right, csh just sits there and sulks...

Another interesting lesson is from the  Sys/VR4  system  that  I  just
installed  at home.  They have this "ttymon" program instead of getty,
and their attitude seems to be that this is God's (or ATT's, which may
be  a synonym ;-) solution to all serial-port problem, and I shouldn't
be such an arrogant idiot as to think that a pipsqueak like  me  could
write  my own daemon.  It has been made clear to me that running uutty
is a total violation of everything, and  that's  why  so  many  things
don't  work.   Actually, with ttymon, all the ports and the mouse were
dead; when I got suspicious and replaced it with uutty, the mouse  and
two  of  the ports started working.  (But they aren't about to give me
any help in fixing the other ports' problems as long  as  I  have  the
gall to run my own weird program on one of the ports.  ;-)

Anyway, uugetty is copyrighted stuff.  If you want  its  capabilities,
you'll  have to find someone willing to write it from scratch and make
it PD or put it under the FSF copyleft.

(If someone has a fax machine or three to loan me;  I  could  probably
add  fax-recognition  code  to  uutty similar to its modem-recognition
kludges - oops - I mean brilliantly-engineered software modules.  ;-)


]
MID: 1992Apr29.201751.10809@nntpd.lkg.dec.com
SUBJ: Re: uugetty in BSD?
BODY: In article <1992Apr21.191506.28334@chinet.chi.il.us>, les@chinet.chi.il.us (Leslie Mikesell) writes: | > >>Well, looking in the 1.03 release of the Taylor-UUCP code, one finds the | > >>[...]No uugetty. | >  | > >Too true. ------------------------------------------------------------
[1992-04-30T16:42:01+00:00] [terry@npd.Novell.COM (Terry Lambert)] [In article <1992Apr29.201751.10809@nntpd.lkg.dec.com> jc@sppip7.lkg.dec.com (John Chambers) writes:
>In article <1992Apr21.191506.28334@chinet.chi.il.us>, les@chinet.chi.il.us (Leslie Mikesell) writes:
>> >>Well, looking in the 1.03 release of the Taylor-UUCP code, one finds the
>> >>[...]No uugetty.
>> 
>> >Too true.  Probably won't ever be one, either.
>> 
>> Too bad. It's time someone added some intelligence to the program to
>> handle  things  like logging caller ID, auto-fax-detection, and even
>> mundane speed switching to match what the modem is  doing  when  the
>> interface speed is not locked.  To do that you need to listen to the
>> modem *before* carrier detect comes up, and if you are  doing  that,
>> you  have  to  coordinate  closely  with  dial-out programs to avoid
>> interferring with them.
>
>Another interesting lesson is from the  Sys/VR4  system  that  I  just
>installed  at home.  They have this "ttymon" program instead of getty,
>and their attitude seems to be that this is God's (or ATT's, which may
>be  a synonym ;-) solution to all serial-port problem, and I shouldn't
>be such an arrogant idiot as to think that a pipsqueak like  me  could
>write  my own daemon.  It has been made clear to me that running uutty
>is a total violation of everything, and  that's  why  so  many  things
>don't  work.   Actually, with ttymon, all the ports and the mouse were
>dead; when I got suspicious and replaced it with uutty, the mouse  and
>two  of  the ports started working.

Actually, ttymon does work.  The intent was to cut down on the number of
processes; it the same reason I wrote 'xportmon'.  If you are running
'xdm' on 36 X terminals at 204K core a pop after shared text and shared
library , you get 7.3 Meg;  260K for one 'xportmon' is a real deal, even
if you don't include distributed authentication.  I know that this
is usually "just swap", but I have a quibble with it -- so sue me.  If
you were to, similarly, have many 'login' programs running (say 128 on
a medium sized box), you would quickly use a large amount of memory (swap)
up.  By using one program to control authentication, this is a scalable
cost based on the number of ports actually in use rather than on the
number potentially in use.  This can be very handy if you run large jobs
at 1 am and have sales and marketing types on during the day.  The tradeoff
is not 100% for users or 100% for batch, as it would have to be otherwise.

The reason it didn't work is that ttymon is not initially configured on
most of the SVr4 comercial releases (including AT&T's for their 6386/E,
6386 WGS, and StarServer configurations).  This is because most users
are more familiar with 'getty' style interfaces and tend to trust them
more, generally out of a fear of things new.

As to caller ID logging, FAX detection, and "mundane" speed switching:
1)	What is the "standard" caller ID mechanism? (trick question).
2)	What is the "standard" fax detection mechanism? (hint: 'nother
trick question).
3)	Auto-bauding requires an intimate knowledge of what a character
at one baud rate looks like at another, and generally requires intimate
knowledge of the hardware involved.  Generally, this is done by looking
for a known character string based on an expected input (say a carriage
return) when dealing with a serial port.  What you do is set the "sample
rate" (baud) as high as possible, then recognize the fact that lower baud
rates will generate character sequences for a known input, each of which
are characteristic of a given baud rate.  This depends on the differences
in clock frequency of the modem and the particualr UART you are using,
which are independant of one another.  Admittedly, these sequences can be
"known" for a particular hardware configuration, but this is not very
efficient or particularly bright.  An easier method would be to let the
modem hardware determine the baud rate and either use the external clocking
portion of the RS232-C standard (which is hardly ever implemented) OR have
the modem communicate it's "CONNECT #####" message at a known baud rate
always.  This seems much simpler, if only because the modem WON'T connect
unless it already KNOWS the baud rate; then the modem switches to the new
baud, the port monitor also switches (due to the message), and the
communications take place normally.  Another less elegant soloution, as
it encourages manufactures to skimp on the buffer space in the modem for
systems like DOS with huge input buffers, since that's their primary
market, is to implement a fixed high baud rate connection between the
modem and computer and to use hardware flow control (CTS/RTS).  The
problem with this is if the modem buffers do not exceed the output
buffers size of the OS times 2 (in the case of UNIX, this tends to be
128 bytes).


>(But they aren't about to give me
>any help in fixing the other ports' problems as long  as  I  have  the
>gall to run my own weird program on one of the ports.  ;-)

The finks! 8-).

>Anyway, uugetty is copyrighted stuff.  If you want  its  capabilities,
>you'll  have to find someone willing to write it from scratch and make
>it PD or put it under the FSF copyleft.

The problem is that you don't really WANT uugetty!  It's perfectly possible
to listen to ports without the carrier detect present.  It's called the
"partial open hack", and has been around for many years!  It's simple:

1)	Post a non-O_NDELAY open to the port and alarm out of it; this
	will "unset" the O_EXCL in the tty struct if you were foolish
	enough to used uugetty, which has two locking mechanisms and
	respects only one (the one it doesn't trust and has to go and
	double check, as it turns out).  This is necessary to allow
	simultaneuos opens due to a fork() to succeed, and required due
	to a 42 line disparity in sys2.c in the dup2() code that causes
	it to deny access to the second fd if O_EXCL is set.  If this
	miraculously succeeds before alarm time, close it.
2)	Post an O_NDELAY open to the port.  This will succeed; don't
	expect it to set your other flags, though, as the flags field of
	the tty struct isn't updated on O_NDELAY opens (hence #1).
3)	Post a blocking open.  This will succeed because the flags field
	is not updated until after the device is "partially open" (hence
	the name of the hack), so it will see the O_NDELAY for the first
	part, but not for the second.
4)	Close the file descriptor from step 2.
5)	You can now use the fd in step three to talk to the modem without
	DCD having to be up, including the posting of reads.

The most common mistake is to set the modem so it ignores DTR, which will
only be raised by the UNIX if the port is open or pending open.  The
modem then answers the phone even if the port isn't enabled.

The second most common mistake is to set the modem so that it always
asserts DCD; this means that the getty is either putting up the initial
"hostname login:" message, or that, due to line noise, login is running
at either "login:" or "password:" prompts.  The problem with this is two
state transition problems in Hayes style modems.  The first is that the
modem will never be reset even if DTR is observed, since with DCD present,
there is no reason for the host to drop DTR.  This results in sessions
living between phone calls and the baud rate "getting stuck" to that of
first call after reset.  The second is that the modem will do the following:

1)	RING RING RING ... etc.
2)	CONNECT 2400 (or whatever)
3)	<raise DCD>

The problem occurs when the computer echoes the message from 2 at the login
prompt before 3 takes place.  The modem say to itself "Hmmmm... I have
carrier; I don't have DCD raised; I am getting characters on my serial port"
and makes the assumption that it's dialing out and that you're trying to
cancel the call.  A human sees "CONNECTED" "NO CARRIER" messages when
dialing in and gets hung up on.


>There are some strict requirements for the port's
>setting in csh, and they aren't documented anywhere, and if you  don't
>get it exactly right, csh just sits there and sulks...

You can find out what these are by doing a "stty -a" or "stty all" on a
port on the machine that works with the native login/getty.



All that said, I have to vote for two devices referring to the same port,
which is the BSD method.  The "callout" device has a higher "priority"
than the "callin" -- the one running getty or ttymon.  Reads and writes
are blocked on the "callin" device while there is an active open on the
"callout" device.

Personal preferences for modification:  I would use an ioctl in getty
and/or login, depending on if your getty was "smart" to say that the
"callout" device was not allowed to be opened.  This would be undone
by the next getty or by init or by the fact that 0 open references
remained (ie: on last close; I like this best).  I would use O_EXCL to
prevent multiple opens to the "callout" device (fixing contention
problems) and apply this flag across the pgrp from the u struct rather
than at the pid, thus fixing the dup2()/fork() problem without having
to change sys2.c.  This resolves all problems that I have seen discussed
so far.

				terry@icarus.weber.edu

[1992-05-13T17:31:32+00:00] [jc@sppip7.lkg.dec.com (John Chambers)] [In article <1992May6.190639.18564@wsrcc.com>, wolfgang@wsrcc.com (Wolfgang S. Rupprecht) writes:
> 
> Normal users shouldn't be able to do anything that will lock up a
> system (or modem), especially not something as simple as:
> 
> 	./run-3-hr-simulation &
> 	logout

Well, I'm probably not a normal user (and if I were normal, I probably
wouldn't be a user ;-), but I've often done just this sort of thing to
*prevent* things from getting locked up.  For instance, on this Ultrix
system,  if  you  use tip to look at a port, it sets the exclusive use
bit, and commands like "stty all >/dev/ttyd0" hang forever  trying  to
open the file.  You can't even kill them with ^D; the window is locked
up until the tip goes away. The folks that invented this exclusive use
stuff  obviously  thought that I was too stupid to be able to make use
of Unix's multiprogramming, even in a simple case like this,  so  they
took steps to prevent me from doing it.

An even better example:  You do a "tip ttyd1" and the modem reports an
incoming call by saying "CONNECT 2400". The serial port is at 9600, so
you have to change it to 2400 to use the connection. You go to another
window and type
   stty 2400 >/dev/ttyd1
and IT HANGS!  Hmmm; tip has an escape mechanism, so you type:
   ~!stty 2400 >/dev/ttyd1
and it doesn't hang, but the connection still gives garbage. Some time
later, you finally diagnose the problem: After the command terminates,
tip reset the port to its "original" state, and it is  back  at  9600.
You  can't win.  (Though your mileage may vary depending on just which
release of which BSD system you are fighting. On some, the ~!stty will
hang, giving you a hung tip, until you reboot.  ;-)

But I have a way of fixing them.  I have a login daemon that  I  wrote
which,  among  other  things, keeps the file permanently open, usually
preventing turkey programs like tip from grabbing it exclusively. I've
found  that  sometimes  they manage to turn on this flag anyway, so my
daemon (which is running as super-user) occasionally checks and  turns
it off.  This makes our multi-programming applications work again.  As
for locking, well, all of mine know how  to  create  (and  delete  ;-)
lockfiles; I wrote myself a routine to do it years ago. Even tip knows
this (though it doesn't bother deleting them).  You can probably guess
what  my  opinion  is  of  anti-social  programs  that  don't use such
(voluntary) lockfiles.  (Give me the source, and I'll rewrite them  so
that they are sociable. ;-)

Of course, I expect a bunch of flames about the  poor  performance  of
using  lockfiles.   Frankly, I consider a hung stty to be a much worse
example of poor performance.  And even worse is when  I  tell  tip  to
exit,  rerun  it,  and  it  says "all ports busy".  If the "efficient"
exclusive-use scheme causes screwups like this, it  is  an  incredible
waste  of  my  time  and the time of a lot of customers who are always
baffled by it.  Such user hostility is inexcusable, and if I  have  to
pay a small performance penalty (e.g., 10 milliseconds rather than 200
microseconds to create a lock) to do locking in a way that won't  give
hung processes, then that's performance that is well wasted.

Also, I expect a bunch of insults saying that I'm obviously too stupid
to understand how exclusive-use works. Don't bother. I'll stipulate up
front that I'm that stupid. I've read TFM, and it seems to make sense,
but  the  code doesn't do what I expect, so clearly I don't understand
it. So I use things that are comprehensible to feeble minds like mine.
I'll  leave such sophisticated features for the more brilliant hackers
who have managed to make sense of them.

--
	To a man who feels, life is a tragedy.
	To a man who thinks, life is a comedy.

[1992-05-13T18:34:12+00:00] [jc@sppip7.lkg.dec.com (John Chambers)] [In article <173@adagio.UUCP>, grog@adagio.UUCP (Greg Lehey) writes:
> 
> >And, alas, SVR4 turned out *not* to be a great unification anyway.
> 
> How about `aggregation'?

Well, personally, I'd object to the "great".

On the 386 machine in my living room, I recently made the  mistake  of
upgrading from 5.3 to 5.4, and I now have a long list of reasons why I
would advise against considering 5.4 in a product.

For example, I have a 4-port serial card which was working  just  fine
as  tty01  thru tty04 (tty00 is a serial mouse on another card).  With
5.4, tty03 and tty04 work just fine. But if any program opens tty01 or
tty02, then all four ports go into a peculiar mode, in which output is
written at the rate of one char every  three  seconds,  and  input  is
never received at all; sometimes tty00 is also disabled.

This wouldn't be so bad if there  were  some  way  of  diagnosing  the
problem.   But  I've  spent several hours talking long-distance to the
field-support people in California,  and  they've  walked  me  through
pretty much the entire kernel build without finding the problem.  "You
must have something configured wrong." Yup, I sure did, but damned  if
anyone  can diagnose the problem.  Perhaps if I had the funds to spend
an unbounded number of dollars buying test equipment and  so  on,  the
problem  may  be  fixable.  Meanwhile, I just describe the problem and
scare off prospective user.

Working as a "consultant" as I do, I find that I can just summarize it
as saying that you will probably just have only the default two usable
serial ports, and the vendor(s) won't be prepared to  handle  anything
as strange and unusual as four or eight ports. If you can make it work
yourself, fine, but don't expect help.  If you  want  more,  go  to  a
vendor with experience with such things.

Note that I haven't named the vendor.  This  is  intentional,  because
I've  exchanged  email  with  several  other  people with very similar
problems with other 5.4 vendors.  It seems to be generic with 5.4; the
serial-port  stuff  was  pretty  much  totally rewritten, they've only
debugged it for 2-port  systems,  and  expertise  from  previous  Unix
systems  is  of  very  little use.  The default configurations for two
ports work well; if you want more, you should wait until they learn to
do it right.

Also, part of what I want to do is to use  the  machine  to  run  some
special-purpose  programs  that talk via a serial port to some special
equipment.  The attitude that I've seen is that on 5.4, there is  this
great  new serial-port daemon called "ttymon", and it's the only thing
that should ever run on a port.  If I have the gall to try running  my
own junk on the port, then all warranties are void.  I should just use
ttymon the way that God intended, and not think that I'm smart  enough
to  write  my own code for talking to serial ports.  So if you want to
use your workstation as a controller for any gadgetry  with  a  serial
interface  (Heath  sells  a  nice little weather station and there are
several packet-radio modems for sale, for examples), forget  5.4;  the
vendors  no  longer  think  that  their system should be used for such
applications.

(Of course, due to the above serial-port problem, I couldn't use *any*
of  the  ports  until I disabled ttymon, but that's another issue from
the one of developing your own serial-port applications.)

[1992-05-15T01:15:18+00:00] [scotty@gagetalker.com (Scott Turner)] [In article <1992May13.183412.9312@nntpd.lkg.dec.com> jc@sppip7.lkg.dec.com

[munch]
|> Also, part of what I want to do is to use  the  machine  to  run  some
|> special-purpose  programs  that talk via a serial port to some special
|> equipment.  The attitude that I've seen is that on 5.4, there is  this

|> own junk on the port, then all warranties are void.  I should just use
|> ttymon the way that God intended, and not think that I'm smart  enough
|> to  write  my own code for talking to serial ports.  So if you want to
|> use your workstation as a controller for any gadgetry  with  a  serial
|> interface  (Heath  sells  a  nice little weather station and there are
|> several packet-radio modems for sale, for examples), forget  5.4;  the
|> vendors  no  longer  think  that  their system should be used for such
|> applications.
It sounds like you are using a four port card with a shared IRQ. The slowness
you describe indicates to me that the kernel has failed to clear the IRQ
before clearing the IRQ controller. As a reward for this the IRQ controller
then shutdown the IRQ.

There are a couple ways that come to mind to fix this:

1. Use FAS, 5.4 still supports non-Streams serial port drivers. There's
also a new call that you can add support for that allows use of poll(2).

2. Write your own Streams driver. :)

3. Write your own IRQ service "fixer" that grabs the IRQ away from the
serial driver and deals with clearing the IRQ properly (polls the serial
ports and feeds them to the asy irq service routine.)

If you switch back to using standard serial ports then you will find that
5.4 serial I/O isn't impossible. 5.4 has no sense of humor about the modem
control lines. You either need to tie them out of the way or make damn sure
you have the serial driver configured to ignore them. Once this is done
the 5.4 drivers work like the 5.3 drivers. The drivers DO work, I've used
them to drive several "odd" devices.

Scotty

[1992-05-20T17:55:52+00:00] [karl@NeoSoft.com (Karl Lehenbauer)] [In article <11225.9205200938@thor.cf.ac.uk> spedpr@thor.cf.ac.uk (Paul Richards) writes:
>I'm having trouble getting 386bsd to mount filesystems. I'm trying to
>mount /home from my sun 3/60 onto /home on my pc. 
>I'm using my own ethernet driver for the Isolink (Bicc 4110-2) card. I don't
>know if this is the cause or not?

I take it you have brought the system up multiuser, and you have some
nfs daemons running and such?  (i.e. /etc/rc has been executed)

We can mount either way from 386BSD to our big server.

Of course you should be suspicious of your isolink driver.  Could you borrow
a supported card, like an 8003 and try that?

[1992-06-04T14:27:49+00:00] [rwd@CS.CMU.EDU (Randall Dean)] [
Announcing the first release of BNR2SS, a Mach 3.0 single
server with environment, based on the Berkeley Network
Release 2 (BNR2) and Bill Jolitz's 386BSD release.

This release contains:

 o  Complete sources to the BNR2SS.   This is a license
    free BSD server which runs on the free Mach 3.0 kernel.  At
    this time, the only platform being supported is the
    INTEL 80x86.
    This first release will also be a SUP only release.  This
    will limit the release to sites with existing experience
    with the Mach 3.0 (or Mach 2.5) system.  In future releases,
    we will make the BNR2SS source available for anonymous FTP.
    We currently make the MACH3 kernel available via FTP.
    Presently, you need the standard Mach build environment
    that is used to build UX and MK, to build BNR2SS

 o  A binary release of the Jolitz 386BSD environment plus a
    Mach 3.0 kernel, BNR2SS, emulator, mach_init, ps and a few
    other programs.
    You can SUP the binary release onto a fresh disk, following the
    recipe below, and boot this system.

All BNR2SS sources and MACH 3.0 sources in this release are free.
All binaries in this release are believed free.  Almost all binaries
were obtained from an anonymous ftp site that housed the 386BSD
binaries and sources.  The sources and binaries in this release
contain one or many of the copyrights found at the end of this
file

Work is currently in progress to make the BNR2 server self-hosting.
This entails moving away from the CMU build environment to
the "free" OSF1 build environment.  A preliminary version of this has
been done, and should be made available soon.

The current system does not have NFS.  Work is in progress to
make this available next release also.

As this is an alpha release, there are many potential bugs in the
server.  This is work in progress and should be treated as such.
We are looking for people willing to test the system and not
only find bugs, but implement a solution.  A mailing list has
been set up for further discussion of this system.  Contributions
should be sent to:

bnr2ss@cs.cmu.edu

requests to be added to the list should be sent to

bnr2ss-request@cs.cmu.edu

			DIRECTIONS FOR SUP

These directions are from the first user of the system besides me.
These have been known to work at least once.

0. I presume you are using the CMU Tahoe Release.  I presume you
   have a spare disk to load the Jolitz distribution and Mach Single
   Server onto.  And I presume you know about the CMU SUP program.
0a. SUP will be used to upload the release.   SUP tranfers
    the userid and groupid information for a file as ascii text and
    if SUP does not find the a matching entry in your passwd/group
    files, it will not be able to set the userid/groupid of the file
    correctly.  So we need steps 1 and 2 below to make SUP happy.

1. Add the following lines to /etc/passwd
acu:*:4:0:Acu Stuff:/:/dev/null
mach3:*:11:11:Mach Developed Software:/usr/mach3:/bin/csh

1a. Your /etc/passwd should then contain the following
root:INHsdIhO9CIq.:0:10:Charlie &:/:/bin/csh
daemon:*:1:31:The devil himself:/:
bin:*:3:10: Binaries Commands and Source:/:/bin/csh
acu:*:4:0:Acu Stuff:/:/dev/null
games:***:7:2:Games pseudo-user:/usr/games:
mach3:*:11:11:Mach Developed Software:/usr/mach3:/bin/csh
uucp:SET UUCP PASSWD:66:1:UNIX-to-UNIX Copy:/usr/spool/uucppublic:/usr/lib/uucp/uucico

2. Add the following lines to /etc/group
bin2:*:7:root
mach3:*:11:root

2a. Your /etc/group should then contain the following
wheel:*:0:root,rootl
daemon:*:1:daemon
kmem:*:2:root
sys:*:3:root
tty:*:4:root
operator:*:5:root
bin2:*:7:root
bin:*:10:root,games
mach3:*:11:root
staff:*:20:root
guest:*:31:root
ingres:*:74:ingres

3. I have codified the instructions below for a "generic" XYZ type
disk.  You would have either a scsi disk, called "sd" (which is what
I used) or an esdi disk, called "hd".  So where ever you see XYZ1
below, you should substitute "sd1" or "hd1" as appropriate for you.
And where you see XYZ, you use "sd" or "hd". You need to setup the
disk for mach use.  So do a 
	diskutil clobber_my_disk /dev/rXYZ1a

4. Create an A and D parititon.  And maybe an E for user data
	eval `vtoc -m a /dev/rXYZ1c` 4096 512
	eval `vtoc -m d /dev/rXYZ1c` 4096 512
	eval `vtoc -m e /dev/rXYZ1c` 4096 512

5. Mount A on /mnt and D on /mnt/usr
	mount /dev/XYZ1a /mnt
	mkdir /mnt/usr
	mount /dev/XYZ1d /mnt/usr

6. Now you can sup.  Use the following control files:
first    host=x29.mach.cs.cmu.edu hostbase=/bnr2/DIST base=/mnt crypt=<crypt>
bin.root host=x29.mach.cs.cmu.edu hostbase=/bnr2/DIST base=/mnt crypt=<crypt>
bin.usr  host=x29.mach.cs.cmu.edu hostbase=/bnr2/DIST base=/mnt crypt=<crypt> prefix=usr
src.bsdss host=x29.mach.cs.cmu.edu hostbase=/bnr2/DIST base=<your_base> crypt=<crypt> prefix=bsdss

6a.  Crypts must be gotten from us.  This is not meant to limit distribution,
     but help us keep track of who is picking up the code.  Please send
     mail to bnr2ss-request@cs.cmu.edu for crypt.

6b.  Since you must build the server in a Mach build environment at this time,
     you should sup the src.bsdss collection to wherever is convenient on your
     current system.

7. The above sup should finish w/o errors.

8. You must create a paging file (I am setting up 20Meg):
	dd if=/dev/rXYZ1d of=/mnt/usr/pagingfile bs=1024k count=20
	cd /mnt/mach_servers44; ln -s /dev/XYZ1d/pagingfile paging_file

9. Now you must fix the following files in etc:
	/etc/hosts
	/etc/networks
	/etc/netstart
	/etc/fstab
9a. Just grab your favorite host and networks.
9b. netstart is pretty self explanatory, don't forget to add "route add"
    for your gateway.
9c. /etc/fstab is a template.  It is setup for the esdi drive.  If you have
    a scsi drive, just go in
    and "xx" out the esdi and undo the "xx"'s for the sd disk.  NOTE: Due to
    an incompatability between i386 MACH and BSD386 disk partitions.  You
    use "2i" under BSDSS to refer to the mach partition "i".  So the lines
    in /etc/fstab use XYZ2a and XYZ2d to refer to the second disk which
    under mach is really XYZ1a and XYZ1d  -- sigh.

10. Now reboot and bring up BSDSS single user.
	/etc/reboot
10a. At the boot prompt type
	XYZ(1,a)mach.boot -s
	
11. Run fsck which will slightly adjust your filesystem.  Then you have to 
    halt the machine and reboot again because the root fs was written.
11a. Physically reboot the machine
11b. At the boot prompt type and boot multi user.
	XYZ(1,a)mach.boot

12. Well you're up and running.  Or at least, it worked for me.
    The initial screen print out of the login banner may be garbled.
    But you should be able to login.  ROOT, ROOTL, AND ROOTLSH HAVE NO
    PASSWORD.  YOU MIGHT WANT TO GIVE THEM ONE REALLY SOON.  B U T    T H I S
    S T U P I D   S Y S T E M   K E E P S   Y O U R   P A S S W O R D
    I N   C L E A R   T E X T,  S O   D O N ' T   U S E   Y O U R
    F A V O R I T E   O N E.  You will also notice that there are 3
    userid for cmu userid's 997, 998, and 999.  You can either delete
    these or give them a password and if we ever need to login you
    can tell us the password.

13. Operationally, the disk subsystem seems pretty reliable.  I have not
    lost any data and have been using the system a bit.  The network is fast
    but ftp seems to run into a bit of trouble if you turn on hashing.  And
    lastly, ^Z is pretty deadly right now.  If you ^Z something, kill it;
    do not ever try to continue it.  And some programs can not even be
    successfully ^Z'd, "ftp" comes to mind.  Lastly, sometimes output
    flow control does not work and the display stops typeout (over telnet
    connections), try typing a space or return at the display.  If necessary
    you can successfully telnet in and kill -9 the typeout.

14. The binary environment is basically what agate.berkeley.edu distributes
    on 386bsd/floppies/3in/bin.  The differences are noted in /MANIFEST.mach.
    Obviously, a lot of our favorite programs are missing.  We can work on
    this.  Also, note that right now we just have "sh" and not "csh".  So
    you must be careful setting up your password entry.  For example, "rootl"
    can not really login, but "rootlsh" can.

15. A few important facts about the environment.  We expect that you will use
    sup to keep your binaries and sources up to date.  In /sup, there is
    a template SUP control file, named SUPFILE, that indicates everything
    we will allow you to sup.  You should copy this file to MYSUPFILE and
    edit out the comments next to the sup collections that you want.  Then
    you run MYSUPFILE to bring your system up to date.  NOTE: at present
    we do not have "sup" built for this environment -- almost.  So you
    must go back to your mach2.5 or mach3.0 system to do this sup.

	-------------- init.c --------------------

#include <signal.h>
#include <setjmp.h>

#include <sys/types.h>
#include <sys/wait.h>

#include <ttyent.h>
#include <stdio.h>

#include <utmp.h>
#include <sys/file.h>
#include <sys/stat.h>
#include <sys/fcntl.h>

		/* default modes from the kernel */
#define MULTIUSER 0
#define SINGLE 1

#define SHELL "/bin/sh"
#define SHELLARG "-sh"
char *shenv[] = { "HOME=/", "TERM=pc3", "USER=root", 0, 0 } ;

char initarg[30];
jmp_buf buf,restart;
int beIdle;

#define CMDLEN 120

struct ttyinfo  {
	struct ttyinfo *next;
	char ttyname[8];
	char command[CMDLEN];
	int commandpid;
	char window[CMDLEN];
	int windowpid;
 } *tty;

int mode = MULTIUSER;		/* assume the kernel wants multiuser */

main(argc, argv)
int argc;
char *argv[];
{
	int n;
	int off = 0;

	beIdle = 0;		/* SIGTSTP */
	/* did some idiot try to run us? */
	if(getpid() != 1) {
		write(2,"System daemon, runnable only by system\n",  39);
		exit(0xff);
	}

	tty = (struct ttyinfo *) 0;		/* no lines running getty */

	/* look for system's argument */
	if(argc > 1 && argv[1][1] == 's') {
		mode = SINGLE;
		strcpy(initarg, "INITARG=");
		strcat(initarg, argv[1]);
		shenv[3] = initarg;
	}

	/* allocate a session for init */
	(void) setsid();

	/* protect against signals, listen for children */
	signal (SIGTERM, SIG_IGN);
	signal (SIGTSTP, SIG_IGN);
	signal (SIGTTIN, SIG_IGN);
	signal (SIGTTOU, SIG_IGN);
	signal (SIGCHLD, SIG_IGN);

	/* close any open files */
	for(n=0; n < 20; n++) close(n);

	if (mode == MULTIUSER)
		multiuser();
	while (1) {
		single();
		multiuser();		/* attempt to go multiuser */
	}
}
		/* locate all lines that should be running a windowing
		   system and start these programs */
loadtty()
{
	struct ttyinfo *t, *p;
	struct ttyent *ttyEntry;
	int found, pid ;

	beIdle = 0;		/* reset the Idle mode */
	while (ttyEntry = getttyent()) {
	   found = 0;
	   for ( t = tty; t;  t = t->next) {
		if (!strncmp(ttyEntry->ty_name, t->ttyname, 8)) {
		   if (ttyEntry->ty_status & TTY_ON) {
				/* make sure the getty and window command did
				   not change -- restart if necessary */
			if (ttyEntry->ty_window) {
		 	    if (strncmp(ttyEntry->ty_window, t->window, CMDLEN))
				strncpy(t->window, ttyEntry->ty_window, CMDLEN);
			    if (! t->windowpid)
				t->windowpid = startcmd(t->window, t->ttyname);
			}
			if (ttyEntry->ty_getty) {
		 	    if (strncmp(ttyEntry->ty_getty, t->command, CMDLEN))
				strncpy(t->command, ttyEntry->ty_getty, CMDLEN);
			    if (! t->commandpid) {
				if (logout(t->ttyname)) logwtmp(t->ttyname,"","");
				t->commandpid = startcmd(t->command,t->ttyname);
			    }
			}
		   }
		   else {
		/* no longer a valid tty -- kill but can't remove */
		      if ((pid = t->windowpid) > 2)  {
			t->windowpid = 0;
		        kill(pid, SIGKILL); /* kill off bad line */
		      }
		      if ((pid = t->commandpid) > 2)  {
			t->commandpid = 0;
			if (logout(t->ttyname)) logwtmp(t->ttyname,"","");
		        kill(pid, SIGKILL); /* kill off bad line */
		      }
		   }
		   found = 1;
		   break;
		}
		p = t;
	   }
		/* add new entry */
	   if (!found && (ttyEntry->ty_status & TTY_ON) && ttyEntry->ty_getty) {
		t = (struct ttyinfo *) malloc(sizeof(struct ttyinfo));
		if (tty)
			p->next = t;
		else
			tty = t;
		   
		t->commandpid = t->windowpid = 0;
		strncpy(t->ttyname,ttyEntry->ty_name, 8);
		if (!ttyEntry->ty_window)
			t->window[0] = '\0';
		else {
			strncpy(t->window, ttyEntry->ty_window, CMDLEN);
			t->windowpid = startcmd(t->window, t->ttyname);
		}
		if (!ttyEntry->ty_getty)
			t->command[0] = '\0';
		else {
			strncpy(t->command, ttyEntry->ty_getty, CMDLEN);
			t->commandpid = startcmd(t->command, t->ttyname);
		}
	   }
	}
	endttyent();
}

	/* start the window or getty command (returns the pid) */
startcmd(command, ttyname)
char *command, *ttyname;
{
	int pid,argc;
	char *cp, *op;
	char buff[CMDLEN], *argv[32];

	if (!(op = command) || ! *op)
		return(0);

	/* break "command arg1 .. argN" into "command" "arg1" .. "argN" */
	cp = buff;
	while (*op && *op != ' ') *cp++ = *op++;
	*cp++ = '\0';
	if (*op == ' ') op++;
	argv[0] = "-";
	for (argc = 1; argc < 32 && *op; argc++) {
		argv[argc] = cp;
		while (*op && *op != ' ') *cp++ = *op++;
		*cp++ = '\0';
		if (*op == ' ') op++;
	}

/* last command is the tty name. will the following break X ? */
	if (argc < 32 && (op = ttyname)) {
		argv[argc++] = cp;
		while (*op && *op != ' ') *cp++ = *op++;
		*cp++ = '\0';
	}
	argv[argc] = (char *) 0;	/* terminate the args */

	if (!(pid = fork())) {	/* child */
		signal (SIGTERM, SIG_DFL);
		signal (SIGHUP, SIG_DFL);
		signal (SIGTSTP, SIG_DFL);

		execvp(buff, argv);
		sleep(30);		/* maybe the windowing system is still
					   coming up */
		exit(0xff);
	}
	return (pid); 		/* parent */
}

catchAlarm()
{
	/* some processes did not die */
	struct ttyinfo  *t;
	
	signal (SIGALRM, SIG_IGN);
		/* I wanted to free, but it caused the system to freeze */
	for (t=tty; t; t=t->next) {
		if (logout(t->ttyname)) logwtmp(t->ttyname,"","");
		t->commandpid = t->windowpid = 0;
	}

#ifdef non
	if (beIdle) {
		beIdle = 0;		/* gets done any way */
		longjmp(restart,0);	/* jump back and redo rc */
	}
#endif
	longjmp(buf, 1);	/* jump back and exit multi-user */

}

	/* exit from multi-user mode */
shutdown()
{
	int i;
				/* eeeeek */
	signal (SIGALRM, catchAlarm);
	alarm(30);	/* in case the kill hangs on bad processes */
	kill (-1, SIGTERM);	/* kill everything */

	for (i = 0; i< 5; i++) {
		kill(-1, SIGKILL); 
	}
	alarm(0);	/* stop the alarm */
	catchAlarm();	/* no return */
}

doIdle()
{
	beIdle = 1;
	return;
}

noIdle()
{
	beIdle = 0;
	return;
}

/* single user mode */
single()
{
	int stat, pid;
		/* make a process */
	signal (SIGTERM, shutdown);
	signal (SIGHUP, noIdle);
	signal (SIGTSTP, doIdle);
	if (setjmp(buf)){	/* exit back to main */
		sleep(10);		/* in case reboot/halt killed the shell */
		return;	/* rc failed */
	}
	if ((pid = fork()) == 0) {
	
		/* signals, to default state */
		signal (SIGTERM, SIG_DFL);
		signal (SIGHUP, SIG_DFL);
		signal (SIGTSTP, SIG_DFL);

			/* clean off console */
		revoke("/dev/console");

			/* do open and configuration of console */
		login_tty(open("/dev/console", 2));

			/* all ready to execute the shell */
		execle(SHELL, SHELLARG, (char *) 0, shenv);

			/* oops */
		write(2, "execle failed: /bin/sh\n", 24);
		exit(0xff);
	}
	while (wait(&stat) != pid) ;
	sleep(10);		/* in case reboot/halt killed the shell */
}


	/* multi-user mode (stopped if /etc/rc failed or SIGTERM */
multiuser()
{
	struct ttyinfo *t;
	int pid;
	int status = 0;

	setjmp(restart);	/* my guess of restarting SIGTSTP/SIGTERM */
	beIdle = 0;		/* SIGTSTP */
	signal (SIGTERM, shutdown);
	signal (SIGHUP, loadtty);
	signal (SIGTSTP, doIdle);
	if (setjmp(buf))	/* exit back to main */
		return;	/* rc failed */
	if (!(pid = fork())) {

		/* signals, to default state */
		signal (SIGTERM, SIG_DFL);
		signal (SIGHUP, SIG_DFL);
		signal (SIGTSTP, SIG_DFL);
	
			/* clean off console */
		revoke("/dev/console");
	
			/* do open and configuration of console */
		login_tty(open("/dev/console", 2));

			/* all ready to execute the shell */
		if (mode == MULTIUSER)
			execle(SHELL,SHELL,"/etc/rc","autoboot",(char *) 0,shenv);
		else
			execle(SHELL, SHELL, "/etc/rc", (char *) 0, shenv);

			/* oops */
		fputs("execle failed: /etc/rc\n",stderr);
		exit(0xff);
	}
	while (wait(&status) != pid) ;
	if (status) {
		sleep(10);	/* in case reboot/halt killed the shell */
		return;	/* rc failed */
	}
	loadtty();		/* start gettys */
	while (1) {
		pid = wait(&status);
		for ( t = tty; t;  t = t->next) {
			if (t->windowpid == pid)
			  if (beIdle)
			   t->windowpid = 0;	/* do not restart */
			  else
			   t->windowpid = startcmd(t->window, t->ttyname);
			else if (t->commandpid == pid) {
			  if (logout(t->ttyname)) logwtmp(t->ttyname,"","");
			  if (beIdle)
			   t->commandpid = 0;	/* do not restart */
			  else
			   t->commandpid = startcmd(t->command, t->ttyname);
			}
		}
	}
}

[1992-06-12T01:07:29+00:00] [cgd@agate.berkeley.edu (Chris G. Demetriou)] [/* I told Bill Jolitz a few days ago that I'd post something about 0.1,
 * as he doesn't really have the time... I barely do too, but i've
 * seen enough discussions about FP emulation to make me puke, and
 * enough requests about 0.1 that i'm finally going to post this...
 *

0.1

Status:
	Being beta-tested.  Trying to kill all the stoopidbugs.
	(Still not perfect, has a few *really* annoying bugs, some
	of which have real impact on continued operation.  Hopefully
	most, if not all of these, will be gone by the release.)

	Bill had wanted to get 0.1 out last Friday.  As you can tell,
	it didn't quite make it.  There are about 6-8 people betatesting
	it right now.  I have some diffs i want to get to him before
	it's released.

New/fun/fixed features:

* A working init.  (yeah, when i got it down on the hard drive, it
	actually booted multiuser the first time... 8-)

* Floating point emulation.  That's right, "We don't need no stinkin'
	math chips!"  (The code that i've seen was taken from Linux...)

* I've not seen a spontaneous vm_fault in ages.

* There are so many bug fixes it's unbelievable.

* UUCP, and RCS.  They're mucho useful...

I could go on, for a long time, but by now you get the picture.
I'm really happy with what i've seen so far.


Bugs as of what i have:

* under heavy load (i.e. much paging/swapping), things tend to sleep
	on vm pages, and never be awakened.  this is evil.

* the com port driver didn't work (it was unchanged from the original).
	I'm trying to get Bill to include the one modified by
	Daniel Muntz, then rivers@ponds.uucp... (I'm also hacking
	SunOS-style bidirectional ports into it...)

* there's a bug with the tty status character on *my* machine
	which can crash the machine if you hold it down for a screen
	and a half, and/or hit it during the "right part" of a disk
	access.

* general annoying things:
	* permissions on some things wrong

	* no DES in libc (and therefore, unencrypted passwords -- Bill
	  doesn't want to distribute crypt.c, for what sound like good
	  reasons to me... i'll be providing him and/or the net with a
	  set of unstructions to fix that...).

	* things like the nfs daemons starting automatically, and
	  then dumping core in /, if NFS is not compiled into the
	  kernel.  (i'm providing a fix for this, which also adds
	  kernel version-numbering back into the system, as well as
	  SunOS-style kernel identification...  hopefully this will
	  be incorporated as well...)

and now the answer to the question you've all been asking:

WHEN?

well, that's a bit tougher.  By all indications, it should have been
out *months* ago, and *I* actually thought that it was going to make
it out over the last weekend...

However, there are a number of things to fix before it gets released.

I feel safe saying that it'll be out within 2 weeks.
(read that as:  If it's not out within 2 weeks, i'm going to be really
annoyed, because i was told when i got this beta that 0.1 would be
out *REALLY* soon... and that was a week ago... 8-)


Hope that helps some of you out...

Chris
cgd@agate.berkeley.edu

This is a general problem with the installation diskettes, I've already
reported to Bill Jolitz. The problem is, those diskettes store their info
into subdirs of root (bin and usr, I guess) when installing }inormally with
tar. They better should have been installed onto two symlinks pointing to
root dir... But, help with:

	mv /usr/usr /usr/nonsense
	mv /usr/nonsense /nonsense
	rmdir /usr
	mv /nonsense /usr

I'm not sure, there are different file systems... If this fails, at least
that should work (but it'll take longer):

	mv /usr/usr/* /usr
	rmdir /usr/usr
