X/*
X * Declare these as initialized data so we can patch them.
X */
Xint	nswbuf = 0;
X#ifdef	NBUF
Xint	nbuf = NBUF;
X#else
Xint	nbuf = 0;
X#endif
X#ifdef	BUFPAGES
Xint	bufpages = BUFPAGES;
X#else
Xint	bufpages = 0;
X#endif
Xint	msgbufmapped;		/* set when safe to use msgbuf */
X
X/*
X * Machine-dependent startup code
X */
Xint boothowto = 0, Maxmem = 0;
Xlong dumplo;
Xint physmem, maxmem;
Xextern int bootdev;
X#ifdef SMALL
Xextern int forcemaxmem;
X#endif
Xint biosmem;
X
Xextern cyloffset;
X
Xcpu_startup(firstaddr)
X	int firstaddr;
X{
X	register int unixsize;
X	register unsigned i;
X	register struct pte *pte;
X	int mapaddr, j;
X	register caddr_t v;
X	int maxbufs, base, residual;
X	extern long Usrptsize;
X	vm_offset_t minaddr, maxaddr;
X	vm_size_t size;
X
X	/*
X	 * Initialize error message buffer (at end of core).
X	 */
X
X	/* avail_end was pre-decremented in pmap_bootstrap to compensate */
X	for (i = 0; i < btoc(sizeof (struct msgbuf)); i++)
X		pmap_enter(pmap_kernel(), msgbufp, avail_end + i * NBPG,
X			   VM_PROT_ALL, TRUE);
X	msgbufmapped = 1;
X
X#ifdef KDB
X	kdb_init();			/* startup kernel debugger */
X#endif
X	/*
X	 * Good {morning,afternoon,evening,night}.
X	 */
X	/*printf(version);
X	printf("real mem  = %d\n", ctob(physmem));*/
X
X	/*
X	 * Allocate space for system data structures.
X	 * The first available real memory address is in "firstaddr".
X	 * The first available kernel virtual address is in "v".
X	 * As pages of kernel virtual memory are allocated, "v" is incremented.
X	 * As pages of memory are allocated and cleared,
X	 * "firstaddr" is incremented.
X	 * An index into the kernel page table corresponding to the
X	 * virtual memory address maintained in "v" is kept in "mapaddr".
X	 */
X
X	/*
X	 * Make two passes.  The first pass calculates how much memory is
X	 * needed and allocates it.  The second pass assigns virtual
X	 * addresses to the various data structures.
X	 */
X	firstaddr = 0;
Xagain:
X	v = (caddr_t)firstaddr;
X
X#define	valloc(name, type, num) \
X	    (name) = (type *)v; v = (caddr_t)((name)+(num))
X#define	valloclim(name, type, num, lim) \
X	    (name) = (type *)v; v = (caddr_t)((lim) = ((name)+(num)))
X	valloc(callout, struct callout, ncallout);
X#ifdef SYSVSHM
X	valloc(shmsegs, struct shmid_ds, shminfo.shmmni);
X#endif
X	/*
X	 * Determine how many buffers to allocate.
X	 * Use 10% of memory for the first 2 Meg, 5% of the remaining
X	 * memory. Insure a minimum of 16 buffers.
X	 * We allocate 1/2 as many swap buffer headers as file i/o buffers.
X	 */
X	if (bufpages == 0)
X		if (physmem < (2 * 1024 * 1024))
X			bufpages = physmem / 10 / CLSIZE;
X		else
X			bufpages = ((2 * 1024 * 1024 + physmem) / 20) / CLSIZE;
X	if (nbuf == 0) {
X		nbuf = bufpages / 2;
X		if (nbuf < 16)
X			nbuf = 16;
X	}
X	if (nswbuf == 0) {
X		nswbuf = (nbuf / 2) &~ 1;	/* force even */
X		if (nswbuf > 256)
X			nswbuf = 256;		/* sanity */
X	}
X	valloc(swbuf, struct buf, nswbuf);
X	valloc(buf, struct buf, nbuf);
X
X	/*
X	 * End of first pass, size has been calculated so allocate memory
X	 */
X	if (firstaddr == 0) {
X		size = (vm_size_t)(v - firstaddr);
X		firstaddr = (int)kmem_alloc(kernel_map, round_page(size));
X		if (firstaddr == 0)
X			panic("startup: no room for tables");
X		goto again;
X	}
X	/*
X	 * End of second pass, addresses have been assigned
X	 */
X	if ((vm_size_t)(v - firstaddr) != size)
X		panic("startup: table size inconsistency");
X	/*
X	 * Allocate a submap for exec arguments.  This map effectively
X	 * limits the number of processes exec'ing at any time.
X	 */
X	exec_map = kmem_suballoc(kernel_map, &minaddr, &maxaddr,
X				 16*NCARGS, TRUE);
X	/*
X	 * Allocate a submap for physio
X	 */
X	phys_map = kmem_suballoc(kernel_map, &minaddr, &maxaddr,
X				 VM_PHYS_SIZE, TRUE);
X
X	/*
X	 * Finally, allocate mbuf pool.  Since mclrefcnt is an off-size
X	 * we use the more space efficient malloc in place of kmem_alloc.
X	 */
X	mclrefcnt = (char *)malloc(NMBCLUSTERS+CLBYTES/MCLBYTES,
X				   M_MBUF, M_NOWAIT);
X	bzero(mclrefcnt, NMBCLUSTERS+CLBYTES/MCLBYTES);
X	mb_map = kmem_suballoc(kernel_map, (vm_offset_t)&mbutl, &maxaddr,
X			       VM_MBUF_SIZE, FALSE);
X	/*
X	 * Initialize callouts
X	 */
X	callfree = callout;
X	for (i = 1; i < ncallout; i++)
X		callout[i-1].c_next = &callout[i];
X
X	/*printf("avail mem = %d\n", ptoa(vm_page_free_count));*/
X
X	/*
X	 * Set up CPU-specific registers, cache, etc.
X	 */
X	initcpu();
X
X	/*
X	 * Set up buffers, so they can be used to read disk labels.
X	 */
X	bufinit();
X
X	/*
X	 * Configure the system.
X	 */
X	configure();
X}
X
X#ifdef PGINPROF
X/*
X * Return the difference (in microseconds)
X * between the  current time and a previous
X * time as represented  by the arguments.
X * If there is a pending clock interrupt
X * which has not been serviced due to high
X * ipl, return error code.
X */
X/*ARGSUSED*/
Xvmtime(otime, olbolt, oicr)
X	register int otime, olbolt, oicr;
X{
X
X	return (((time.tv_sec-otime)*60 + lbolt-olbolt)*16667);
X}
X#endif
X
Xstruct sigframe {
X	int	sf_signum;
X	int	sf_code;
X	struct	sigcontext *sf_scp;
X	sig_t	sf_handler;
X	int	sf_eax;	
X	int	sf_edx;	
X	int	sf_ecx;	
X	struct	sigcontext sf_sc;
X} ;
X
Xextern int kstack[];
X
X/*
X * Send an interrupt to process.
X *
X * Stack is set up to allow sigcode stored
X * in u. to call routine, followed by kcall
X * to sigreturn routine below.  After sigreturn
X * resets the signal mask, the stack, and the
X * frame pointer, it returns to the user
X * specified pc, psl.
X */
Xvoid
Xsendsig(catcher, sig, mask, code)
X	sig_t catcher;
X	int sig, mask;
X	unsigned code;
X{
X	register struct proc *p = curproc;
X	register int *regs;
X	register struct sigframe *fp;
X	struct sigacts *ps = p->p_sigacts;
X	int oonstack, frmtrap;
X
X	regs = p->p_regs;
X        oonstack = ps->ps_onstack;
X	frmtrap = curpcb->pcb_flags & FM_TRAP;
X	/*
X	 * Allocate and validate space for the signal handler
X	 * context. Note that if the stack is in P0 space, the
X	 * call to grow() is a nop, and the useracc() check
X	 * will fail if the process has not already allocated
X	 * the space with a `brk'.
X	 */
X        if (!ps->ps_onstack && (ps->ps_sigonstack & sigmask(sig))) {
X		fp = (struct sigframe *)(ps->ps_sigsp
X				- sizeof(struct sigframe));
X                ps->ps_onstack = 1;
X	} else {
X		if (frmtrap)
X			fp = (struct sigframe *)(regs[tESP]
X				- sizeof(struct sigframe));
X		else
X			fp = (struct sigframe *)(regs[sESP]
X				- sizeof(struct sigframe));
X	}
X
X	if ((unsigned)fp <= USRSTACK - ctob(p->p_vmspace->vm_ssize)) 
X		(void)grow((unsigned)fp);
X
X	if (useracc((caddr_t)fp, sizeof (struct sigframe), B_WRITE) == 0) {
X		/*
X		 * Process has trashed its stack; give it an illegal
X		 * instruction to halt it in its tracks.
X		 */
X		SIGACTION(p, SIGILL) = SIG_DFL;
X		sig = sigmask(SIGILL);
X		p->p_sigignore &= ~sig;
X		p->p_sigcatch &= ~sig;
X		p->p_sigmask &= ~sig;
X		psignal(p, SIGILL);
X		return;
X	}
X
X	/* 
X	 * Build the argument list for the signal handler.
X	 */
X	fp->sf_signum = sig;
X	fp->sf_code = code;
X	fp->sf_scp = &fp->sf_sc;
X	fp->sf_handler = catcher;
X
X	/* save scratch registers */
X	if(frmtrap) {
X		fp->sf_eax = regs[tEAX];
X		fp->sf_edx = regs[tEDX];
X		fp->sf_ecx = regs[tECX];
X	} else {
X		fp->sf_eax = regs[sEAX];
X		fp->sf_edx = regs[sEDX];
X		fp->sf_ecx = regs[sECX];
X	}
X	/*
X	 * Build the signal context to be used by sigreturn.
X	 */
X	fp->sf_sc.sc_onstack = oonstack;
X	fp->sf_sc.sc_mask = mask;
X	if(frmtrap) {
X		fp->sf_sc.sc_sp = regs[tESP];
X		fp->sf_sc.sc_fp = regs[tEBP];
X		fp->sf_sc.sc_pc = regs[tEIP];
X		fp->sf_sc.sc_ps = regs[tEFLAGS];
X		regs[tESP] = (int)fp;
X		regs[tEIP] = (int)((struct pcb *)kstack)->pcb_sigc;
X	} else {
X		fp->sf_sc.sc_sp = regs[sESP];
X		fp->sf_sc.sc_fp = regs[sEBP];
X		fp->sf_sc.sc_pc = regs[sEIP];
X		fp->sf_sc.sc_ps = regs[sEFLAGS];
X		regs[sESP] = (int)fp;
X		regs[sEIP] = (int)((struct pcb *)kstack)->pcb_sigc;
X	}
X}
X
X/*
X * System call to cleanup state after a signal
X * has been taken.  Reset signal mask and
X * stack state from context left by sendsig (above).
X * Return to previous pc and psl as specified by
X * context left by sendsig. Check carefully to
X * make sure that the user has not modified the
X * psl to gain improper priviledges or to cause
X * a machine fault.
X */
Xsigreturn(p, uap, retval)
X	struct proc *p;
X	struct args {
X		struct sigcontext *sigcntxp;
X	} *uap;
X	int *retval;
X{
X	register struct sigcontext *scp;
X	register struct sigframe *fp;
X	register int *regs = p->p_regs;
X
X
X	fp = (struct sigframe *) regs[sESP] ;
X
X	if (useracc((caddr_t)fp, sizeof (*fp), 0) == 0)
X		return(EINVAL);
X
X	/* restore scratch registers */
X	regs[sEAX] = fp->sf_eax ;
X	regs[sEDX] = fp->sf_edx ;
X	regs[sECX] = fp->sf_ecx ;
X
X	scp = fp->sf_scp;
X	if (useracc((caddr_t)scp, sizeof (*scp), 0) == 0)
X		return(EINVAL);
X#ifdef notyet
X	if ((scp->sc_ps & PSL_MBZ) != 0 || (scp->sc_ps & PSL_MBO) != PSL_MBO) {
X		return(EINVAL);
X	}
X#endif
X        p->p_sigacts->ps_onstack = scp->sc_onstack & 01;
X	p->p_sigmask = scp->sc_mask &~
X	    (sigmask(SIGKILL)|sigmask(SIGCONT)|sigmask(SIGSTOP));
X	regs[sEBP] = scp->sc_fp;
X	regs[sESP] = scp->sc_sp;
X	regs[sEIP] = scp->sc_pc;
X	regs[sEFLAGS] = scp->sc_ps;
X	return(EJUSTRETURN);
X}
X
Xint	waittime = -1;
X
Xboot(arghowto)
X	int arghowto;
X{
X	register long dummy;		/* r12 is reserved */
X	register int howto;		/* r11 == how to boot */
X	register int devtype;		/* r10 == major of root dev */
X	extern char *panicstr;
X
X	howto = arghowto;
X	if ((howto&RB_NOSYNC) == 0 && waittime < 0 && bfreelist[0].b_forw) {
X		register struct buf *bp;
X		int iter, nbusy;
X
X		waittime = 0;
X		(void) splnet();
X		printf("syncing disks... ");
X		/*
X		 * Release inodes held by texts before update.
X		 */
X		if (panicstr == 0)
X			vnode_pager_umount(NULL);
X		sync((struct sigcontext *)0);
X
X		for (iter = 0; iter < 20; iter++) {
X			nbusy = 0;
X			for (bp = &buf[nbuf]; --bp >= buf; )
X				if ((bp->b_flags & (B_BUSY|B_INVAL)) == B_BUSY)
X					nbusy++;
X			if (nbusy == 0)
X				break;
X			printf("%d ", nbusy);
X			DELAY(40000 * iter);
X		}
X		if (nbusy)
X			printf("giving up\n");
X		else
X			printf("done\n");
X		DELAY(10000);			/* wait for printf to finish */
X	}
X	splhigh();
X	devtype = major(rootdev);
X	if (howto&RB_HALT) {
X		printf("halting (in tight loop); hit reset\n\n");
X		splx(0xfffd);	/* all but keyboard XXX */
X		for (;;) ;
X	} else {
X		if (howto & RB_DUMP) {
X			dumpsys();	
X			/*NOTREACHED*/
X		}
X	}
X#ifdef lint
X	dummy = 0; dummy = dummy;
X	printf("howto %d, devtype %d\n", arghowto, devtype);
X#endif
X	reset_cpu();
X	for(;;) ;
X	/*NOTREACHED*/
X}
X
Xint	dumpmag = 0x8fca0101;	/* magic number for savecore */
Xint	dumpsize = 0;		/* also for savecore */
X/*
X * Doadump comes here after turning off memory management and
X * getting on the dump stack, either when called above, or by
X * the auto-restart code.
X */
Xdumpsys()
X{
X
X	if (dumpdev == NODEV)
X		return;
X	if ((minor(dumpdev)&07) != 1)
X		return;
X	dumpsize = physmem;
X	printf("\ndumping to dev %x, offset %d\n", dumpdev, dumplo);
X	printf("dump ");
X	switch ((*bdevsw[major(dumpdev)].d_dump)(dumpdev)) {
X
X	case ENXIO:
X		printf("device bad\n");
X		break;
X
X	case EFAULT:
X		printf("device not ready\n");
X		break;
X
X	case EINVAL:
X		printf("area improper\n");
X		break;
X
X	case EIO:
X		printf("i/o error\n");
X		break;
X
X	default:
X		printf("succeeded\n");
X		break;
X	}
X	printf("\n\n");
X	DELAY(1000);
X}
X
Xmicrotime(tvp)
X	register struct timeval *tvp;
X{
X	int s = splhigh();
X
X	*tvp = time;
X	tvp->tv_usec += tick;
X	while (tvp->tv_usec > 1000000) {
X		tvp->tv_sec++;
X		tvp->tv_usec -= 1000000;
X	}
X	splx(s);
X}
X
Xphysstrat(bp, strat, prio)
X	struct buf *bp;
X	int (*strat)(), prio;
X{
X	register int s;
X	caddr_t baddr;
X
X	/*
X	 * vmapbuf clobbers b_addr so we must remember it so that it
X	 * can be restored after vunmapbuf.  This is truely rude, we
X	 * should really be storing this in a field in the buf struct
X	 * but none are available and I didn't want to add one at
X	 * this time.  Note that b_addr for dirty page pushes is 
X	 * restored in vunmapbuf. (ugh!)
X	 */
X	baddr = bp->b_un.b_addr;
X	vmapbuf(bp);
X	(*strat)(bp);
X	/* pageout daemon doesn't wait for pushed pages */
X	if (bp->b_flags & B_DIRTY)
X		return;
X	s = splbio();
X	while ((bp->b_flags & B_DONE) == 0)
X		sleep((caddr_t)bp, prio);
X	splx(s);
X	vunmapbuf(bp);
X	bp->b_un.b_addr = baddr;
X}
X
Xinitcpu()
X{
X}
X
X/*
X * Clear registers on exec
X */
Xsetregs(p, entry)
X	struct proc *p;
X	u_long entry;
X{
X
X	p->p_regs[sEBP] = 0;	/* bottom of the fp chain */
X	p->p_regs[sEIP] = entry;
X
X	p->p_addr->u_pcb.pcb_flags = 0;	/* no fp at all */
X	load_cr0(rcr0() | CR0_EM);	/* start emulating */
X#ifdef	NPX
X	npxinit(0x262);
X#endif
X}

X#define	GNULL_SEL	0	/* Null Descriptor */
X#define	GCODE_SEL	1	/* Kernel Code Descriptor */
X#define	GDATA_SEL	2	/* Kernel Data Descriptor */
X#define	GLDT_SEL	3	/* LDT - eventually one per process */
X#define	GTGATE_SEL	4	/* Process task switch gate */
X#define	GPANIC_SEL	5	/* Task state to consider panic from */
X#define	GPROC0_SEL	6	/* Task state process slot zero and up */
X#define NGDT 	GPROC0_SEL+1
X
Xunion descriptor gdt[GPROC0_SEL+1];
X
X/* interrupt descriptor table */
Xstruct gate_descriptor idt[32+16];
X
X/* local descriptor table */
Xunion descriptor ldt[5];
X#define	LSYS5CALLS_SEL	0	/* forced by intel BCS */
X#define	LSYS5SIGR_SEL	1
X
X#define	L43BSDCALLS_SEL	2	/* notyet */
X#define	LUCODE_SEL	3
X#define	LUDATA_SEL	4
X/* seperate stack, es,fs,gs sels ? */

X

X/* software prototypes -- in more palitable form */
Xstruct soft_segment_descriptor gdt_segs[] = {
X	/* Null Descriptor */
X{	0x0,			/* segment base address  */
X	0x0,			/* length - all address space */
X	0,			/* segment type */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor priority level */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor present */
X	0,0,
X	0,			/* default 32 vs 16 bit size */
X	0  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ },
X	/* Code Descriptor for kernel */
X{	0x0,			/* segment base address  */
X	0xfffff,		/* length - all address space */
X	SDT_MEMERA,		/* segment type */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor priority level */
X	1,			/* segment descriptor present */
X	0,0,
X	1,			/* default 32 vs 16 bit size */
X	1  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ },
X	/* Data Descriptor for kernel */
X{	0x0,			/* segment base address  */
X	0xfffff,		/* length - all address space */
X	SDT_MEMRWA,		/* segment type */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor priority level */
X	1,			/* segment descriptor present */
X	0,0,
X	1,			/* default 32 vs 16 bit size */
X	1  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ },
X	/* LDT Descriptor */
X{	(int) ldt,			/* segment base address  */
X	sizeof(ldt)-1,		/* length - all address space */
X	SDT_SYSLDT,		/* segment type */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor priority level */
X	1,			/* segment descriptor present */
X	0,0,
X	0,			/* unused - default 32 vs 16 bit size */
X	0  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ },
X	/* Null Descriptor - Placeholder */
X{	0x0,			/* segment base address  */
X	0x0,			/* length - all address space */
X	0,			/* segment type */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor priority level */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor present */
X	0,0,
X	0,			/* default 32 vs 16 bit size */
X	0  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ },
X	/* Panic Tss Descriptor */
X{	(int) &panic_tss,		/* segment base address  */
X	sizeof(tss)-1,		/* length - all address space */
X	SDT_SYS386TSS,		/* segment type */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor priority level */
X	1,			/* segment descriptor present */
X	0,0,
X	0,			/* unused - default 32 vs 16 bit size */
X	0  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ },
X	/* Proc 0 Tss Descriptor */
X{	(int) kstack,			/* segment base address  */
X	sizeof(tss)-1,		/* length - all address space */
X	SDT_SYS386TSS,		/* segment type */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor priority level */
X	1,			/* segment descriptor present */
X	0,0,
X	0,			/* unused - default 32 vs 16 bit size */
X	0  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ }};

X	0,			/* segment descriptor present */

X	0  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ },
X	/* Null Descriptor - overwritten by call gate */
X{	0x0,			/* segment base address  */
X	0x0,			/* length - all address space */

X	0,			/* segment descriptor priority level */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor present */
X	0,0,
X	0,			/* default 32 vs 16 bit size */
X	0  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ },
X	/* Null Descriptor - overwritten by call gate */
X{	0x0,			/* segment base address  */

X	0,			/* segment descriptor priority level */
X	0,			/* segment descriptor present */

X	0  			/* limit granularity (byte/page units)*/ },
X	/* Code Descriptor for user */
X{	0x0,			/* segment base address  */

X/* table descriptors - used to load tables by microp */
Xstruct region_descriptor r_gdt = {
X	sizeof(gdt)-1,(char *)gdt
X};
X
Xstruct region_descriptor r_idt = {
X	sizeof(idt)-1,(char *)idt
X};
X
Xsetidt(idx, func, typ, dpl) char *func; {
X	struct gate_descriptor *ip = idt + idx;
X
X	ip->gd_looffset = (int)func;
X	ip->gd_selector = 8;
X	ip->gd_stkcpy = 0;
X	ip->gd_xx = 0;
X	ip->gd_type = typ;
X	ip->gd_dpl = dpl;
X	ip->gd_p = 1;
X	ip->gd_hioffset = ((int)func)>>16 ;
X}
X
X#define	IDTVEC(name)	__CONCAT(X, name)
Xextern	IDTVEC(div), IDTVEC(dbg), IDTVEC(nmi), IDTVEC(bpt), IDTVEC(ofl),
X	IDTVEC(bnd), IDTVEC(ill), IDTVEC(dna), IDTVEC(dble), IDTVEC(fpusegm),
X	IDTVEC(tss), IDTVEC(missing), IDTVEC(stk), IDTVEC(prot),
X	IDTVEC(page), IDTVEC(rsvd), IDTVEC(fpu), IDTVEC(rsvd0),
X	IDTVEC(rsvd1), IDTVEC(rsvd2), IDTVEC(rsvd3), IDTVEC(rsvd4),
X	IDTVEC(rsvd5), IDTVEC(rsvd6), IDTVEC(rsvd7), IDTVEC(rsvd8),
X	IDTVEC(rsvd9), IDTVEC(rsvd10), IDTVEC(rsvd11), IDTVEC(rsvd12),
X	IDTVEC(rsvd13), IDTVEC(rsvd14), IDTVEC(rsvd14), IDTVEC(syscall);
X
Xint lcr0(), lcr3(), rcr0(), rcr2();
Xint _udatasel, _ucodesel, _gsel_tss;
X
Xinit386(first)
X{       extern ssdtosd(), lgdt(), lidt(), lldt(), etext; 
X	int x, *pi, i;
X	unsigned char *iobitmap;
X
X	unsigned biosbasemem, biosextmem;
X	struct gate_descriptor *gdp;
X	extern int sigcode,szsigcode;
X
X	proc0.p_addr = proc0paddr;
X
X	/*
X	 * Initialize the console before we print anything out.
X	 */
X
X	cninit (KERNBASE+0xa0000);
X
X	/* make gdt memory segments */
X	gdt_segs[GCODE_SEL].ssd_limit = btoc((int) &etext + NBPG);
X	for (x=0; x < NGDT; x++) ssdtosd(gdt_segs+x, gdt+x);
X	/* make ldt memory segments */
X	ldt_segs[LUCODE_SEL].ssd_limit = btoc(UPT_MIN_ADDRESS);
X	ldt_segs[LUDATA_SEL].ssd_limit = btoc(UPT_MIN_ADDRESS);
Xldt_segs[LUDATA_SEL].ssd_limit = 0xfffff; /* pace for x */
X
X	/* Note. eventually want private ldts per process */
X	for (x=0; x < 5; x++) ssdtosd(ldt_segs+x, ldt+x);
X
X	/* exceptions */
X	setidt(0, &IDTVEC(div),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(1, &IDTVEC(dbg),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(2, &IDTVEC(nmi),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X 	setidt(3, &IDTVEC(bpt),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_UPL);
X	setidt(4, &IDTVEC(ofl),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(5, &IDTVEC(bnd),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(6, &IDTVEC(ill),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(7, &IDTVEC(dna),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(8, &IDTVEC(dble),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(9, &IDTVEC(fpusegm),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(10, &IDTVEC(tss),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(11, &IDTVEC(missing),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(12, &IDTVEC(stk),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(13, &IDTVEC(prot),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(14, &IDTVEC(page),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(15, &IDTVEC(rsvd),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(16, &IDTVEC(fpu),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(17, &IDTVEC(rsvd0),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(18, &IDTVEC(rsvd1),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(19, &IDTVEC(rsvd2),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(20, &IDTVEC(rsvd3),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(21, &IDTVEC(rsvd4),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(22, &IDTVEC(rsvd5),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(23, &IDTVEC(rsvd6),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(24, &IDTVEC(rsvd7),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(25, &IDTVEC(rsvd8),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(26, &IDTVEC(rsvd9),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(27, &IDTVEC(rsvd10),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(28, &IDTVEC(rsvd11),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(29, &IDTVEC(rsvd12),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(30, &IDTVEC(rsvd13),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X	setidt(31, &IDTVEC(rsvd14),  SDT_SYS386TGT, SEL_KPL);
X
X#include	"isa.h"
X#if	NISA >0
X	isa_defaultirq();
X#endif
X
X	lgdt(gdt, sizeof(gdt)-1);
X	lidt(idt, sizeof(idt)-1);
X	lldt(GSEL(GLDT_SEL, SEL_KPL));
X
X	/* Use BIOS values stored in RTC CMOS RAM, since probing
X	 * breaks certain 386 AT relics.
X	 */
X	biosbasemem = rtcin(RTC_BASELO)+ (rtcin(RTC_BASEHI)<<8);
X	biosextmem = rtcin(RTC_EXTLO)+ (rtcin(RTC_EXTHI)<<8);
X
X	/* if either bad, just assume base memory */
X	if (biosbasemem == 0xffff || biosextmem == 0xffff) {
X		maxmem = min (maxmem, 640/4);
X	} else if (biosextmem > 0 && biosbasemem == 640) {
X		int pagesinbase, pagesinext;
X
X		pagesinbase = 640/4 - first/NBPG;
X		pagesinext = biosextmem/4;
X		/* use greater of either base or extended memory. do this
X		 * until I reinstitue discontigous allocation of vm_page
X		 * array.
X		 */
X		if (pagesinbase > pagesinext)
X			Maxmem = 640/4;
X		else {
X			Maxmem = pagesinext + 0x100000/NBPG;
X			first = 0x100000; /* skip hole */
X		}
X	}
X	maxmem = Maxmem - 1;	/* highest page of usable memory */
X	physmem = maxmem;	/* number of pages of physmem addr space */
X
X	/* call pmap initialization to make new kernel address space */
X	pmap_bootstrap (first, 0);
X	/* now running on new page tables, configured,and u/iom is accessible */
X
X	/* make a initial tss so microp can get interrupt stack on syscall! */
X	proc0.p_addr->u_pcb.pcb_tss.tss_esp0 = (int) kstack + UPAGES*NBPG;
X	proc0.p_addr->u_pcb.pcb_tss.tss_ss0 = GSEL(GDATA_SEL, SEL_KPL) ;
X        for (i=0,iobitmap= &proc0.p_addr->u_pcb.pcb_tss.tss_iomap;
X	     i <1022;i++) {
X	  iobitmap[i] = 0;
X	}
X        iobitmap[1023] = 0xf;
X        proc0.p_addr->u_pcb.pcb_tss.tss_ioopt[1]= sizeof(tss) - 1024;
X
X	_gsel_tss = GSEL(GPROC0_SEL, SEL_KPL);
X	ltr(_gsel_tss);
X
X	/* make a call gate to reenter kernel with */
X	gdp = &ldt[LSYS5CALLS_SEL].gd;
X	
X	x = (int) &IDTVEC(syscall);
X	gdp->gd_looffset = x++;
X	gdp->gd_selector = GSEL(GCODE_SEL,SEL_KPL);
X	gdp->gd_stkcpy = 0;
X	gdp->gd_type = SDT_SYS386CGT;
X	gdp->gd_dpl = SEL_UPL;
X	gdp->gd_p = 1;
X	gdp->gd_hioffset = ((int) &IDTVEC(syscall)) >>16;
X
X	/* transfer to user mode */
X
X	_ucodesel = LSEL(LUCODE_SEL, SEL_UPL);
X	_udatasel = LSEL(LUDATA_SEL, SEL_UPL);
X
X	/* setup proc 0's pcb */
X	bcopy(&sigcode, proc0.p_addr->u_pcb.pcb_sigc, szsigcode);
X	proc0.p_addr->u_pcb.pcb_flags = 0;
X	proc0.p_addr->u_pcb.pcb_ptd = IdlePTD;
X}
X
Xextern struct pte	*CMAP1, *CMAP2;
Xextern caddr_t		CADDR1, CADDR2;
X/*
X * zero out physical memory
X * specified in relocation units (NBPG bytes)
X */
Xclearseg(n) {
X
X	*(int *)CMAP2 = PG_V | PG_KW | ctob(n);
X	load_cr3(rcr3());
X	bzero(CADDR2,NBPG);
X	*(int *) CADDR2 = 0;
X}
X
X/*
X * copy a page of physical memory
X * specified in relocation units (NBPG bytes)
X */
Xcopyseg(frm, n) {
X
X	*(int *)CMAP2 = PG_V | PG_KW | ctob(n);
X	load_cr3(rcr3());
X	bcopy((void *)frm, (void *)CADDR2, NBPG);
X}
X
X/*
X * copy a page of physical memory
X * specified in relocation units (NBPG bytes)
X */
Xphyscopyseg(frm, to) {
X
X	*(int *)CMAP1 = PG_V | PG_KW | ctob(frm);
X	*(int *)CMAP2 = PG_V | PG_KW | ctob(to);
X	load_cr3(rcr3());
X	bcopy(CADDR1, CADDR2, NBPG);
X}
X
X/*aston() {
X	schednetisr(NETISR_AST);
X}*/
X
Xsetsoftclock() {
X	schednetisr(NETISR_SCLK);
X}
X
X/*
X * insert an element into a queue 
X */
X#undef insque
X_insque(element, head)
X	register struct prochd *element, *head;
X{
X	element->ph_link = head->ph_link;
X	head->ph_link = (struct proc *)element;
X	element->ph_rlink = (struct proc *)head;
X	((struct prochd *)(element->ph_link))->ph_rlink=(struct proc *)element;
X}
X
X/*
X * remove an element from a queue
X */
X#undef remque
X_remque(element)
X	register struct prochd *element;
X{
X	((struct prochd *)(element->ph_link))->ph_rlink = element->ph_rlink;
X	((struct prochd *)(element->ph_rlink))->ph_link = element->ph_link;
X	element->ph_rlink = (struct proc *)0;
X}
X
Xvmunaccess() {}
X
X/*
X * Below written in C to allow access to debugging code
X */
Xcopyinstr(fromaddr, toaddr, maxlength, lencopied) u_int *lencopied, maxlength;
X	void *toaddr, *fromaddr; {
X	int c,tally;
X
X	tally = 0;
X	while (maxlength--) {
X		c = fubyte(fromaddr++);
X		if (c == -1) {
X			if(lencopied) *lencopied = tally;
X			return(EFAULT);
X		}
X		tally++;
X		*(char *)toaddr++ = (char) c;
X		if (c == 0){
X			if(lencopied) *lencopied = tally;
X			return(0);
X		}
X	}
X	if(lencopied) *lencopied = tally;
X	return(ENAMETOOLONG);
X}
X
Xcopyoutstr(fromaddr, toaddr, maxlength, lencopied) u_int *lencopied, maxlength;
X	void *fromaddr, *toaddr; {
X	int c;
X	int tally;
X
X	tally = 0;
X	while (maxlength--) {
X		c = subyte(toaddr++, *(char *)fromaddr);
X		if (c == -1) return(EFAULT);
X		tally++;
X		if (*(char *)fromaddr++ == 0){
X			if(lencopied) *lencopied = tally;
X			return(0);
X		}
X	}
X	if(lencopied) *lencopied = tally;
X	return(ENAMETOOLONG);
X}
X
Xcopystr(fromaddr, toaddr, maxlength, lencopied) u_int *lencopied, maxlength;
X	void *fromaddr, *toaddr; {
X	u_int tally;
X
X	tally = 0;
X	while (maxlength--) {
X		*(u_char *)toaddr = *(u_char *)fromaddr++;
X		tally++;
X		if (*(u_char *)toaddr++ == 0) {
X			if(lencopied) *lencopied = tally;
X			return(0);
X		}
X	}
X	if(lencopied) *lencopied = tally;
X	return(ENAMETOOLONG);
X}
END-of-/tmp/machdep.c
echo x - /tmp/tss.h
sed 's/^X//' >/tmp/tss.h << 'END-of-/tmp/tss.h'

X/*
X * Intel 386 Context Data Type
X */

Xstruct i386tss {
X	int	tss_link;	/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	int	tss_esp0; 	/* kernel stack pointer priviledge level 0 */
X#define	tss_ksp	tss_esp0
X	int	tss_ss0;	/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	int	tss_esp1; 	/* kernel stack pointer priviledge level 1 */
X	int	tss_ss1;	/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	int	tss_esp2; 	/* kernel stack pointer priviledge level 2 */
X	int	tss_ss2;	/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	/* struct  ptd *tss_cr3; 	/* page table directory */
X	int	tss_cr3; 	/* page table directory */
X#define	tss_ptd	tss_cr3
X	int	tss_eip; 	/* program counter */
X#define	tss_pc	tss_eip
X	int	tss_eflags; 	/* program status longword */
X#define	tss_psl	tss_eflags
X	int	tss_eax; 
X	int	tss_ecx; 
X	int	tss_edx; 
X	int	tss_ebx; 
X	int	tss_esp; 	/* user stack pointer */
X#define	tss_usp	tss_esp
X	int	tss_ebp; 	/* user frame pointer */
X#define	tss_fp	tss_ebp
X	int	tss_esi; 
X	int	tss_edi; 
X	int	tss_es;		/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	int	tss_cs;		/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	int	tss_ss;		/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	int	tss_ds;		/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	int	tss_fs;		/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	int	tss_gs;		/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	int	tss_ldt;	/* actually 16 bits: top 16 bits must be zero */
X	unsigned char tss_ioopt[2];	/* options & io offset bitmap: currently zero */
X   			/* XXX unimplemented .. i/o permission bitmap */
X	unsigned char tss_iomap[1024]; /* hack i/o permission bitmap */
X};
END-of-/tmp/tss.h

[1992-06-26T01:46:05+00:00] [terry@npd.Novell.COM (Terry Lambert)] [In article <Jun.25.10.13.41.1992.17965@pilot.njin.net> pfaffman@pilot.njin.net (Jay Pfaffman) writes:
>
>I'm trying to learn more about how NFS clients work.  I've found
>several different sources for servers, but I'm looking for the client
>stuff.  I assume that it's somewhere in the kernel & maybe in mount?
>Poking around the source tree at gatekeeper.dec.com didn't uncover
>anything.  If someone could point me to the right directories/files or
>offer other sources of info, I'd appreciate it.

Most modern NFS clients are implemented as filesystems under the VFS layer.
If you archie for "nfs_vnops.c" you will see a number of locations around
the world where you may pick the code up.

There are several notable exceptions to this, where the client is not
implemented below VFS, or only partially implemented there:

1)	NFS for SVR3.1.  The implementation I am most familiar with is
	implemented as a file system switch entry, several system calls,
	and a user-space nfs client daemon.  This is because RPC service
	is unavailable from the kernel (3.1 doesn't support streams and/
	or calls to streams from the kernel).

2)	The Kyoto NFS, which is implemented in much the same way, but does
	have a VFS table entry, but goes on with the system calls, et al,
	just as if it were SVR3.1.  The Berkeley interface, which differs
	significantly in the vnops and the kernel vm API from SVR4, is
	used in this case.


Most likely, unless you are using a real BSD or derivative (Older SunOS/
Ultrix), or unless you wanted to use all of 386BSD (my recommendation), you
will probably need a SVR4.x source license to see working code... unless,
God forbid, ypu have an AT&T UNIX PC (the "7300", sometimes called a "3B1"),
in which case, you will have to have a SVR3.1 src license and get AT&T to
sell you the other sources for whatever they think they can get out of you
(last time I checked, they wanted $200 for "Rogue" ported to the 3b1).


					Terry Lambert
					terry_lambert@gateway.novell.com
					terry@icarus.weber.edu

[1992-07-06T15:32:58+00:00] [eric@snark.thyrsus.com (Eric S. Raymond)] [Archive-name: pc-unix/software
Last-update: Mon Jul  5 11:49:45 EDT 1992
Version: 5.0

You say you want cutting-edge hacking tools without having to mortgage the
wife'n'kids?  You say arrogant workstation vendors are getting you down?  You
say you crave fast UNIX on cheap hardware, but you don't know how to go about
getting it?  Well, pull up a chair and take the load off yer feet, bunky,
because...

This is v5.0 of the PC-clone UNIX Software Buyer's Guide posting
current to July 5th 1992.

******************************************************************************
*                                                                            *
* DON'T PANIC...                                                             *
*        DO NOT ATTEMPT TO ADJUST YOUR NEWSREADER...                         *
*               THE HARDWARE BUYER'S GUIDE & USL BUG LIST *ARE* STILL ALIVE! *
*                                     see below...                           *
*                                                                            *
******************************************************************************

What's new in this issue:
   * Straight from inside Dell, info on the new Dell 2.2
   * Technical info on the new 4.0.4 releases from Dell, Esix, and Microport.
   * Known bugs in Dell UNIX.
   * Numerous minor corrections and additions.

Gentle Reader: if you end up buying something based on information from this
Guide, please do yourself and the net a favor; make a point of telling the
vendor "Eric's FAQ sent me" or some equivalent.  The idea isn't to hype me
personally, I've already got all the notoriety I need from doing things like
_The_New_Hacker's_Dictionary_ --- but if we can show vendors that the Guide
influences a lot of purchasing decisions, I can be a more powerful advocate for
the net's interests, and for you.

0. CONTENTS

  I.  INTRODUCTION.  What this posting is.  How to help improve it.  Summary of
the 386/486 UNIX market, including 6 SVr4 vendors and 2 BSD ports.  What's new
in this issue.

  II.  GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS.  A brief discussion of general hardware
requirements and compatibility considerations in the base SVR4 code from UNIX
Systems Laboratories (referred to below as the USL code).  None of this
automatically applies to SCO or the two BSD-like versions, which break out the
corresponding information into their separate vendor reports.

  III. FEATURE COMPARISON. A feature table which gives basic price & feature
info and summarizes differences between the versions.

  IV. VENDOR REPORTS.  Detailed descriptions of the different versions and
vendors, including information collected from the net on bugs, supported
and unsupported hardware and the like.

  V. HARDWARE COMPATIBILITY TABLES.  A set of tables summarizes vendor claims
and user reports on hardware compatibility.

  VI. FREE ADVICE TO VENDORS.  Your humble editor's soapbox.  An open letter
to the UNIX vendors designed to get them all hustling to improve their products
and services as fast as possible.

  VII. ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS AND ENVOI.  Credit where credit is due.  Some praises
and pans.  What comes next....

Note: versions 1.0 through 4.0 of this posting had a different archive name
(386-buyers-faq) and included the following now separate FAQs as sections.

  pc-unix/hardware -- (formerly HOT TIPS FOR HARDWARE BUYERS) Useful general
tips for anybody buying clone hardware for a UNIX system.  Overview of the
market.  Technical points.  When, where, and how to buy.

  usl-bugs -- (formerly KNOWN BUGS IN THE USL CODE).  A discussion of bugs
known or believed to be generic to the USL code, with indications as to which
porting houses have fixed them.  None of this applies to the two BSD-based
versions.


I. INTRODUCTION

The purpose of this posting is to pool public knowledge and USENET feedback
about all leading-edge versions of UNIX for commodity 386 and 486 hardware.  It
also includes extensive information on how to buy cheap clone hardware to
support your UNIX.

This document is maintained and periodically updated as a service to the net by
Eric S.  Raymond <eric@snark.thyrsus.com>, who began it for the very best
self-interested reason that he was in the market and didn't believe in plonking
down several grand without doing his homework first (no, I don't get paid for
this, though I have had a bunch of free software and hardware dumped on me as a
result of it!).  Corrections, updates, and all pertinent information are
welcomed at that address.

This posting is periodically broadcast to the USENET group comp.unix.sysv386
and to a list of vendor addresses.  If you are a vendor representative, please
check the feature chart and vendor report to make sure the information on your
company is current and correct.  If it is not, please email me a correction
ASAP.  If you are a knowledgeable user of any of these products, please send me
a precis of your experiences for the improvement of the feedback sections.

At time of writing, here are the products in this category:

Consensys UNIX Version 1.3			abbreviated as "Cons" below
Dell UNIX Issue 2.2				abbreviated as "Dell" below
ESIX System V Release 4.0.4			abbreviated as "Esix" below
Micro Station Technology SVr4 UNIX		abbreviated as "MST" below
Microport System V Release 4.0 version 4	abbreviated as "uPort" below
UHC Version 3.6					abbreviated as "UHC" below

SCO Open DeskTop 1.1				abbreviated as "ODT" below

BSD/386	(0.3 beta)				abbreviated as "BSDI" below
Mach386						abbreviated as "Mach" below

Note: Dell 2.2 is scheduled for release in July.  It includes several
significant improvements over 2.1, which it replaces.  All information in
the Dell column of the tables and vendor report now pertains to 2.2, based
on information received from Jeremy Chatfield at Dell.

The first six of these are ports of USL's System V Release 4.  Until last year
there was a seventh, by Interactive Systems Corporation.  That product was
canned after half of ISC was bought by SunSoft, evidently to clear the decks
for Solaris 2.0 (a SunOS port for the 386 to be released late in 1992).  The
only Interactive UNIX one can buy at present is an SVr3.2 port which I consider
uninteresting because it's no longer cutting-edge; I have ignored it.

Earlier issues ignored SCO because (a) 3.2 isn't leading-edge any more and (b)
their `Version 4' is a 3.2 sailing under false colors.  Can you say deceptive
advertising?  Can you say bait-and-switch?  Can you say total marketroid-puke?
However, the clamor from netters wanting it included was deafening.  The day
SCO landed an unsolicited free copy of ODT on my doorstep I gave in.  I don't
expect to actually use it, but I summarize the relevant facts along with
everything else below.  Note that ODT is their full system with networking and
X windows; what they call SCO UNIX is missing most of those trimmings.

BSD/386 is *not* based on USL code, but on the CSRG NET2 distribution tape.
Complete sources are included with every system shipped!  Mach386 is basically
BSD tools with the monolithic Mach 2.5 kernel and does entail a USL license;
it's based on the Tahoe BSD distribution.  For a few extra bucks, you can
get Mach 3.0 (a true microkernel) with *source*!.

LynxOS is a 386 UNIX specialized for real-time work, available from Lynx
Real-Time Systems Inc. of Los Gatos, California.  It includes TCP/IP, NFS, X,
etc.  Most of the development tools are GNU.  The kernel is pre-emptable and
supports threads and dynamically-loased device drivers.

Siemens AG plans to offer a SVr4 port optimized for real-time work in
June 1992.  This product will be called SORIX.  

AT&T's own 386 UNIX offering is not covered here because it is available and
supported for AT&T hardware only.

All the vendors listed offer a 30-day money-back guarantee, but they'll be
sticky about it except where there's an insuperable hardware compatibility
problem or you trip over a serious bug.  One (UHC) charges a 25% restocking fee
on returns.  BSDI offers a 60-day guarantee and says: "If a customer is
dissatisfied with the product, BSDI unconditionally refunds the purchase
price."  Dell says "30 day money-back guarantee, no questions asked".

There are some freeware alternative UNIXes available for the 386/486.  None of
these are yet complete and mature hacking environments, but they show promise
(and require much less in minimum hardware to run).  They are:

386BSD:
   Under development by Bill & Lynne Jolitz & friends (this is the same 386BSD
project described in Dr. Dobbs' Journal some time back).  This OS is based on
the NET/2 tape from Berkeley and strongly resembles the commercial BSD/386
release described below, and like it is distributed with full source.  The aim
is to produce a full POSIX-compliant freeware BSD UNIX.  At the moment it comes
up single-user in root, and utilities as basic as grep are still missing.
However, the whole kernel and TCP/IP is working (which means the hard part is
done) and development is proceeding rapidly.  There's a lot of traffic in
comp.unix.bsd about this project.

Linux:
   This is a POSIX-emulating UNIX lookalike, being written from scratch and
currently in beta.  At the moment, it's less complete than 386BSD because it
doesn't leverage as much pre-existing code, but the kernel and development
tools are up and usable.  No TCP/IP yet, but they have local sockets for X.
Linux is changing so fast that more description would probably be more
misleading than enlightening.  There's an active linux group on USENET,
comp.os.linux, and a (now less active) linux-activists mailing list; to
subscribe, mail to "linux-activists-requests@niksula.hut.fi".  Up-to-the minute
info is also available by fingering torvalds@kruuna.helsinki.fi.

Hurd:
   This is the long-awaited and semi-mythical GNU kernel.  It's being worked on
by the Free Software Foundation (the people who brought you emacs, gcc, gdb and
the rest of the GNU tool suite) but it's not ready for prime time yet.  It's
said to be a set of processes layered over a Mach 3.0 kernel.  The 386BSD and
Linux developments both lean heavily on GNU tools.

There is one other not-quite-freeware (cheapware?) product that deserves a
mention:

Minix:
   This is a roughly V7-compatible UNIX clone for Intel boxes, sold
with source by Prentice-Hall for $169 (there's an associated book for
a few bucks more).  It's really designed to run in 16-bit mode on 8086
and 286 machines, though the UK's MINIX center offers a 32-bit kernel.
UUCP and netnews clones are available as freeware but not supplied
with the base system.  A large international community is involved in
improving Minix; see comp.os.minix on USENET for details.

These freeware and "cheapware" products exert valuable pressure on the
commercial vendors.  Someday, they may even force AT&T to unlock source to stay
competitive...

Finally, there is a class of commercial UNIX clones that claim to emulate UNIX
or improve on it without being derived from AT&T source.   The leading products
of this kind for 80x86 machines seem to be Coherent and QNX.  The following
information about these has been supplied by various USENETters:

   COHERENT is a small-kernel UNIX-compatible multi-user, multi-tasking
development O/S for $99.95 that uses less than 14Mb of disk space, runs on most
286-386-486 CPU systems, has a 64k limit C compiler and over 230 UNIX commands
including text processing, program development, administrative and maintenance
functions. It resides on a partition separate from DOS and can access the DOS
file system with the DOS command. It has no network or Xwindows support, but
cnews and rn have been ported and it has its own newsgroup, comp.os.coherent.
It is fully documented with both a comprehensive 1200 page manual and an
on-line manual. Mark Williams Company provides excellent support including a
UUCP access BBS and has just announced Release 4.0, the 386 version of
COHERENT.


II. GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS

To run any of this systems, you need at least the following: 4 MB of RAM and
80MB of hard disk (SCO says 8MB minimum for ODT 2.0).  However, this is an
absolute minimum; you'll want at least 8 MB of RAM for reasonable performance.
And depending on options installed, the OS will eat from 40 to 120 meg of the
disk, so you'll want at least 200 meg for real work.  To run X you'll need a
VGA monitor and card, and 12-16MB RAM would be a good idea.

Installation from these systems requires that you boot from a hi-density
floppy (either 3.5" or 5.25").  Most vendors offer the bulk of the system on a
QIC 1/4-inch tape; otherwise you may be stuck with loading over 60 diskettes!
In general, if the initial boot gets far enough to display a request for
the first disk or tape load, you're in good shape.

USL SVr4 conforms to the following software standards: ANSI X3.159-1989 C,
POSIX 1003.1, SVID 3rd edition, FIPS 151-1, XPG3, and System V ABI.  4.0.4
ports conform to the iBCS-2 binary standard.  The SVr4 C compiler (C Issue 5)
includes some non-ANSI extensions (however, note that as of mid-1992, no SVr4
ports other than AT&T's have been formally POSIX-certified).  SCO meets all
these except System V ABI and SVID 3rd edition; however, it does conform to
SVID 2nd edition.

All SVr4 versions include support for BSD-style file systems with 255-character
segment names and fragment allocation.  In general this is a Good Thing, but
some SVr3.2 and XENIX binaries can be confused by the different size of
the inode index.  You need to run these on an AT&T-style file system.  SCO
UNIX 3.2v4 (thus, ODT 2.0 but not 1.1) has an `EAFS' file system which adds
symlinks and long filenames.  Old SCO binaries can be confused by long
filenames.

All SVr4 versions include the UNIX manual pages on-line.  Dell stocks
Prentice-Hall's SVr4 books and will sell them to you with your system (in lieu
of printed manuals) at extra cost.  You can order them direct from
Prentice-Hall at (201)-767-5937.  Warning: they ain't cheap!  Buying the whole
13 volumes will cost you a couple hundred bucks.  Esix, Microport and UHC
have their own manual sets derived from the same AT&T source tapes as the
Prentice-Hall set; Esix charges extra for them, but Microport and UHC both
include them with their systems.

SVr4 includes hooks for a DOS bridge that allows you to run DOS applications
under UNIX (the two products that actually do this are DOS Merge and VP/ix).
Most vendors do not include either of these with the base system, however.

All these systems support up to 1024x768 by 256 color super-VGA under X.  The
640x480 by 16 colors of standard VGA is no problem; everybody supports that
compatibly.  However, X servers older than the Roell or X11R5 version (that is,
MIT X11R4 or anything previous) are hard to configure for the clock timings of
your controller and monitor scan frequency unless you have one of the standard
combinations USL supports or your vendor has configured for it.

There are a couple of known hardware compatibility problems the USL code
doesn't yet address.  See the KNOWN BUGS section near the end of this document.


III. FEATURE COMPARISON

To interpret the table below, bear in mind the following things:

   All these products except BSDI/386, Mach386 and SCO ODT are based on the
SVr4 kernel from UNIX Systems Laboratories (USL), an AT&T spinoff.  Thus they
share over 90% of their code and features.  Product differentiation is done
primarily through support policy, bug-fix quality and add-on software.

   The `USL support?' column refers to the fact that USL support is a separate
charge from the source license.  With the former, a porting house gets access
to AT&T's own OS support people and their bug fix database, and the porting
house's bug fixes can get folded back into the USL code.

   These systems come either in a "crippled" version that supports at most two
simultaneous users, or an unlimited version.  Generally the vendors do allow
you to upgrade your license via a patch disk if your requirements, but this
invariably costs slightly more than the base price difference between 2-user
and unlimited systems.

   The "run-time" system in the price tables below is a minimum installation,
just enough to run binaries.  The "complete" system includes every software
option offered by the vendor; it does *not* bundle in the cost of the
Prentice-Hall docs offered by some vendors as an option.  You may well get
away with less, especially if you're willing to do your own X installation.

   The `Upgrade plan' section refers only to upgrades from previous versions
of the same vendor's software.

   The numbers under support-with-purchase are days counted from date
of shipment.  The intent is to help you get initially up and running.

   The engineer counts below are as supplied by vendors; .5 of an engineer
means someone is officially working half-time.  The `Uses USENET' column is
`yes' if there is allegedly at least one person in the engineering department
who reads USENET technical groups regularly and is authorized to respond to
USENET postings reporting problems.

   A dash `-' means the given feature or configuration is not offered.  A `yes'
means it is currently offered; `soon' means the vendor has represented that it
will be offered in the near future.  A `no' means it's not offered, but there's
some related information in the attached footnote.

    afford the man-hours.
(m) Consensys explicitly refuses to release this information.
(n) There's an $80 media charge for the diskettes equivalent to the normal
    60MB distribution tape.
(o) SCO's own X11R4 implementation (based on Locus's VGA driver code)
(p) At present, you must buy Mach386 Autosupport to get SLIP.
(q) The SCO networking bundle includes PPP, as will ODT 2.0.
(r) Mark Boucher <marc@cam.org> has written a PPP driver for ESIX

The SCO information is included by popular demand for comparison purposes.
In the price figures, the `runtime' system is SCO UNIX 3.24; the `complete'
system is ODT with development tools.

In general, the SVr4 market breaks into two tiers.  The bottom tier is
Consensys and MST; low-ball outfits selling stock USL with minimal support for
real cheap.  The top tier is Dell, Esix, Microport and UHC; these guys are
selling support and significant enhancements and charge varying premiums for
it.  Your first, most basic buying decision has to be which tier best serves
your needs.

One further note: it *is* possible to buy these systems at less than the list
the vendor charges!  I found some really substantial discounts in one
mail-order catalog ("The Programmer's Shop"; call 1-(800)-426-8006 to get on
their mailing list, but be prepared to wade through a lot of DOS cruft).


IV. VENDOR REPORTS
    Vendor reports start here.  Each one is led by a form feed.

   SCO Open DeskTop

VENDOR:

   400 Encinal Street

   1-(800)-347-4381 (customer service and tech support)
